ENTITLED TO A REWARD.—The boys, one named John and the other Willie, were ono day fighting in a public street. John, having got the worst, went away crying, when a gentleman came forward, gave John sixpence, and told him to be quiet. When the gentleman was about going away Willie ran forward and said—" Jock, I should get half of it, for if I had not beaten you, you would not have got it." A MANSFOMIATION. —Scene: Model lodging- house not twenty miles from Paisley. A tramp in luck is cooking a piece of steak over the fire. He leaves it to get ssme salt as seasoning, and on his ?return finds in its place a red herring. " Change- able weather this, Pat," said a man, boiling some potatoes. "Begorra, an' it is moighty changeable. I left as good a sleek as ever man ate in that pan there, and when I came back it has changed into this herring.

AN APPRECIATED COMPLIMENT.—The fat man was going round the lake in the park in imitation of the ruthless scorcher. He was bent over the handle-bars in an attitude peculiar to the hands of a clock at a quarter-past six. Little rivulets coursed down his roly-poly face. 1' Hi, there I" yelled a bicycle policeman, sharply, "you're going more than eight miles an hour." The fat man looked aghast, and then tumbled off his machine in astonishment. "Mr. Officer," he puffed, "if you were not on duty, I'd ask you to have a drink. You've paid me the greatest compliment of my life." " TIIE time has come !" His fond eyes dilated with the excess of love that, long suppressed and held in control, now blazed forth with unrestrained vigour, his rich contralto voice trembling with its weight of happiness ; the young lover turned to- wards the girl whom he had so long been devoted to, and, holding out his arms, he continued, more calmly: "Yes, my darling, I am glad to say that the long hours of waiting, of suspense on your part, and of severe privation and hard work on mine, are at last to be rewarded. Since the happy even- ing, now some eight months past, when I first con- fided to you the passionate love that animated my whole being, and when, in response, you revealed the state of your heart, I have been toiling with one end in view, and that was to place myself in a position where we could be joined together in those bonds which no man dare put asunder. By strict economy during these long weary months of waiting, I have succeeded in amassing a sum which, though not large, is yet sufficient to enable us to take this important stcp." The young man almost imperceptibly tightened his grip, while his face betrayed a shade of anxiety. "Yes, dear," he proceeded, carefully weighing his words; "and yet, when I say sufficient,' I refer to a sum that does not mean any unnecessary display or ostenta- tion. Our wedding must be necessarily a quiet one _say in the afternoon, in the presence of a few friends, the flowers and carriages attendant on a church wedding being rather more than my slender pnrse can afford. I shall not, of course, be able to buy you a wedding present, or take you on a but this immaterial compared is i honeymoon trip; to the great fact that we can get married. And now, toy darling, tell me wii at you think about it." The young girl detached herself thoughtfully from the embrace of the fervent one. "I think, d ear," " she said, simply, her slight form trembling with emotion, "that under the circumstances it might be well to wait three or four years longer."

LADIES' LETTER.

My Thus NELLIE. lb is full early to speak of the new dress materials, but from what can be foreseen, the new colour will be Cyrano, which, although in reality a very, very old one, is what our grandmothers would have called a rich puce. It is a lovely shade, lustrous, yet soft. Reps of every kind are the goods that are being prepared for autumn wear, poplins, wool reps, bengalinee and fine soft silk reps of French manufacture finding first favour.

The best gowns for yachting are those of serge or cloth ; linen skirts are also to be considered. A muslin gown for a girl in mourning might be most admirably achieved in black with an embroidered white spot, a transparent yoke of white lace, and s skirt showing muslin flounces, each one frilled with white lace of the narrowest width and of Valenciennes description. Crown such a costume with a black chip hat lined with white, trimmed with a scarf of black and white spotted chiffon, and the result will be excellent. Another extremely useful dress might be of white flannel trimmed with box-pleats, under which appears a vest of pink velvet.

A pretty grey and black foulard gown which was recently pointed out to me was a dove-grey foulard, with a light foliage design in black. The wide re- vers were faced and double flounced with soft grey silk, and the chemisette was in grey silk with black spots. The becoming hat worn with this toilette was in light grey felt, draped with a stylish ar- rangement of grey silk and black velvet, furnished with a tuft of black ostrich tips and osprey. One of the ktest fashions N a coat and skirt in black open work embroidery made up over light green glacti silk, with an accompanying green Alk blouse, the trimmings all of the same handsome guipure. A large picture hat is worn with it. In the coming season this open work embroidery on a completely new scale and in a novel style will be pretty sure to assert itself. As to headgear a charming hat is in black and white with full niche of white chiffon veiled with black lace, high French plumes fastened with soft ribbon velvet bow and paste buckle. For millinery generally there will be a pronounced taste for Valkyrie wings in leathers and in jet—but in a lesser degree. The chief ornaments for hats are long pins made from Roman pearls set in old silver and studded with imitation diamonds. Also the same article made in hollow and perforated jet and set in old silver studded with diamonds and mounted in tertoiseshell.

The hate which turn up in the front are not having it all their own way. A good many are favouring those which turn down at the back and the front, being curved slightly over each ear; and trimmed either with ostrich feathers or a scarf of some diaphanous material. Muslin dresses in plain colours, in mauve or pale pink for choice, are most attractive when trimmed with Valenciennes lace and headings, threaded with black velvet rib- bons. Then this new order of hat, which is, after all, our old friend "the flop," may be made oi Tuscan or Leghorn wreathed with flowers and tied with ribbons. Such costumes need pretty girls to show them to their best advantage; but, then, pretty girls in England are as plentiful as black- berries in October.

It is now warm enough for the children to wear thin washing pelisses. A charming pelisse might be made of embroidered cambric, lined with w,sh- ing silk, and trimmed with insertions and edgings of tine embroidery. The deep collar is caught with pale satin ribbons, and knots of the same appear at the throat. A gathered frill of embrcidery hangs off the edge, inside which is a frill of silk, edged with lace. With this is worn a straw hat trimmed with pale glac6 silk bows and ends, which are wired at the edges, so that they do not soon get out of condition. Yours lovingly JF.Ntrin.

To CLAN' A MACKINTOSH CLOAK.—Lay the mackintosh flat on a board or table, take a brush used for scrubbing clothes, some lukewarm water in a basin, some plain yellow soap. Thoroughly brush the cloak both right and wrong sides until all dirty marks go, then rinse thoroughly in different lots of cold water and hang it up to dry, but not near a fire. The mackintosh will look nice and as fresh as new.

A WARD OF THE KING.

RT KATHARINE S. MACQUOID, Author of "Maisie Derrick," "The Story of Lois.' "Patty," "Louisa," "At the Red Glove," "Miss Eyon of Eyoncourt," Sc.c.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Jeanne Kergrist, a wealthy heiress and an only child, has become on her father's death ward of the King of France. Jeanne is only thirteen when the King commands her to marry the Count of Laval, who is twenty years her ,senior. The story, opens in the garden of Jeanne's house, where Jeanne tells her friend and playfellow Rolland of her dislike to the proposed marriage. Rolland, a fine young fellow, dislikes it too. He has •.been brought .up with Jeanne, and, if he were richer, would marry her when she grows up. The Count of Laval comes to the castle, accompanied by a handsome young cousin, Gilles de Retz. Jeanne likes the Count, but she takes a dislike to Be Retz, who flatters and pities her. He and the Count of Laval leave the castle directly after the marriage ceremony. Fcur years later, Jeanne's mother dies. Rolland has been away fighting in Italy and the Netherlands. Jeanne is alone in the world, and her husband falls ill. He sends De Retz to fetch his young wife home to Laval. Jeanne is seventeen, and has become beautiful. She travels in a litter to Laval, accom- panied by her maids and her mother's woman Gillonne. a courageous and clever Bretonne. They reach Laval at the end of four days and learn that Jeanne's husband, the Count, is dead. De Retz is uqw Count of Laval, and Gillonne suspects his intentioes towards her mistress. She resolves to seek help and advice from her widowed mistress's friend Rolland, who has now become a Viscount. Jeanne atlics the Count of Laval to take her to Amboise to seek protection horn her guardian, the King. Laval consents. He is determined to marry Jeanne, whose wealth, position and beauty attract him. He is also bent on capturing the Constable of Bourbon, lately declared guilty of high treason. Jeanne and her escort leave Laval. Gillonne quits her mistress on the pretext of seeking a relative, and narrowly escapes, being murdered by two of Laval's soldiers, ,who accompany her. Laval tells Jeanne that the King has left Amboise, and they journey in search of him. By degrees he wins the girl's confidence, and makes her feel ashamed of the prejudice she has taken up against him. They stay at an inn where the hostess secretly warns Jeanne that Laval is an unsafe companion, and tells her that he seeks to betray and capture the Con- stable of Bourbon. The Constable was the dearest friend of Jeanne's father, and this warning of Laval's intentions distresses and alarms the girl. Laval manages to quiet her suspicions, and they travel on together, he says, to find the King at Lyons. But he is really taking her into the heart of the Bourbon country.

CHAPTER XL JEANNE ASKS A QUESTION,

Jeanne de Laval's night proved almost as fatiguing as her day had been. She slept from sheer physical exhaustion, but terrible and exciting dreams caused her constantly to waken in a fever of unrest. Towards morning she fell into a deep sleep. From this she suddenly waked; she was trying to rouse up the Constable de Bourbon, to save him from Laval, who held a dagger at his throat. Tha young noble turned on her; she held him fast, in the hope that the Duke would escape. As she struggled, she beard a faint cry; she seemed to see Rolland de la Touche and Laval fighting over the prostrate body of Charles de Bourbon, and she heard Laval cry: "Treason— treason! I summon you to yield in the King's name!" There was a clapping of hands, and she awoke.

She could not at first recall where she was, or how she came to be lying on this hard bed in the 3emi-darkness. Though the sun had not yet risen, a square of cold blue glimmer chewed at the thinly- curtained window. Jeanne raised herself on one elbow, and she guessed at the cause of her sudden awakening, Inc wind had risen, and it now blew such a gale that the loosened vine branches flapped angrily against the lattice, as though determined to force an entrance.

Heavy breathing from the loft told Jeanne that her women still slumbered; she rose and lo)ked out. All was quiet; but even while she gazed with half-opened eyes at the road that led straight upwards to the town, now faintly appearing in the gloom, some neighbouring cock crew. The sound acted like a spell, just as the Prince's kiss acted on the enchanted surroundings of the Princess in the wood. A calf bleated feebly in its shed; its mother, tethered some way off, answered her weanling with pathetic yearning; the shrill cook crowed again, another answered, and a horse softly whinnied, as if in rebuke to the slumber- breaker. But the spell of silence was broken, the mysterious wakening of dawn had begun; there was a murmur in the air, a sort of throb through the house, that said: "Rest is over; another workday Fur the toilers of the earth has begun." Jeanne put on her garments without rousing up her women; she said her orisons, and then she seated herself by the window. She drew forward one of the curtains, so that she could watch the road without beine scan from without. Sitting there, she thought over her hostess's strange story. Jeanne felt very calm and collected, and she wondered at herself. What had happened to change her thus quickly from a child to a woman? It was hard to believe that so short a while ago she was playing, a wild, frolicsome girl, in the garden at Longuedec. She supposed that her great sorrow and then her sudden widowhood must have changed her. "I do not look like a widow," she thought, "but it is not my fault. My dear mother bade me go dressed as a bride should be to Laval." While she thought over the hostess's story, she remembered talk she had heard in childhood. She knew that King Francis and the Duke, his cousin, had never been dear friends. The King considered the Constable assmnptive an 4 severe; he also con- sidered his following and way of living .far too stately and magnificent for a subject. Jeanne had heard that the Constable censured the Ring for weakly yielding to the Queen-Mother's false and impolitic advice; he also deplored the vanity by which Francis had more than once lost a battle and the lives of many distinguished captains and brave soldiers, because he had insisted on com- manding in person, instead of trusting the issue to more experienced leaders. While she sat thinking, she noticed a slight sound below the window; then the tall, lithe figure of Laval came forth from the house; he looked around him, and followed the road into Moulins. He was alone, and on foot, but he wore back and breast pieces, and his long sword hung at his side. It seemed strange to the girl that ho should go forth unattended. Presently she saw a figure appear at the top of the rising road; it was a man, and he came forward to meet Laval. The young noble quickened his steps, and as soon as the two met they. turned back together towards the town. Jeanne asked herself if this was the traitor the hostess had spoken of. She had, however, no chance of satisfy- ing herself on this head. In spite of the stir she had fancied, not a voice as yet could be heard in the house. The light, however, became rapidly more definite, though it was hard to say when the sun rose, so heavy and gloomy was the cloudy sky. Then all at once the house seemed to wake up; there were, feet on the creaking stairs ; there were voices that called and those that answered. Horses' feet stamped on the stones of the courtyard below Jeanne 's chamber; Anne and Mathurine came in to wait on their mistress, and wondered at finding her ready without their help. Madame Vougeon appeared with food and wino for the lady, and with orders to make ready for immediate departure. "Is there such haste? " the Countess asked. "I was to tell madame that the horses arc bridled and saddled ; the leader is to pin the party a lengue,or so further on the road.

ifhe Woman left the room abruptly; she seethed to have forgotten her last night's talk with the lady. She only asked how the Countess had slept. Jeanne and her women soon appeared in the courtyard ~• a man came forward to help the lady mount, and Jeanne recognised Gaspard. "Where did you leave my woman, Gillonne ? she said sternly. "Why has she not come back with you P" Gaspard bowed with deep reverence. "Madame's woman is with her cousin at •lavenne ; she wished to linger there, so we came on here for fresh orders. I bade her go on to Amboise, and wait, there for madame." Jeanne did not answer; she was greatly surprised that Gillonne had not returned with her escort. She looked round to bid adieu to Madamo Vougeon, but the woman had remained standing in the doorway that led into the courtyard, seem- ingly observing a flight of pigeons crossing from roof to roof of the half-timbered building. The instant that Gaspard left the Countess and mounted his own horse, the hostess came forward.

• pressed up to the lady, wad asked leave to kiss her I hand.

"Madame has a long journey before her," she said, sa that anyone might hear, "and she will see the finest view is on the of the road." Jeanne bowed, and with Gaspard at their head the party l'eft, the courtyard of the hostelry, For the first part of the way, Jeanne was fully occupied in meditating on the task which possibly lay before her; a possibility sternly recalled by the hostess's parting words. She scarcely noticed the country, lying grey and still in the early morn- ing light. They stopped at,a tolerable-looking inn for dijegner, and Laval suddenly appeared in its doorway, as if he had fallen from the skies—and, indeed, he looked so glowing that the comparison is in harmony. The soldier Gaspard had told his leader the same story about Gillanne that he had reported to the woman's mistress, and Laval now expressed his regret that the Countess should be deprived of her woman's services. He was secretly incensed by Gaspard's news; he hoped the man would have understood his hint, and would have rid himself in a surer fashion of the duenna. Jeanne scarcely answered Laval; she had some- thing more serious than Gillonne's absence to talk about. He Was struck by the calmness of her manner as she said: "Monsieur, with your leave, I wish to tia: question, but I will wait till your attention is lair_ likely to be called away from ine than it is now, when you have only just joined is." He you at her so admiringly that she grew uneasy under the gaze of his long narrow dark eyes.. "Madame does herself less than justice if she thinks that anything in this expedition weighs nearly so much with me as her goodwill and her pleasure in all matters." He bowed low, and waited for her to speak, without offering to help her dismount. Jeanne glanced right and left; no one was near enough to make out her words; this seemed to be the opportunity she sought. "You, monsieur, doubtless know what passes in the world, and can therefore enlighten my ignorance." She paused, and Laval smiled wit genuine pleasure, for he had fancied this proud maiden despised him. He looked so handsome, so anxious to please her, that Jeanne refused to believe he could be engaged in this despicable hunt for a brave man. "Command me, madame, I beseech Toil, in anything that lies in 9, power to answer.' "I wish to know'—.she affected a careless manner "whether my lord the Constable of Bourbon is at Lyons with his Majesty " She looked full at him, and his eyes shifted Under her direct glance. "Will madame dismount and eat? I can thee tell her all I know during the meal." He helped her to dismount with more than usual care, and 'receded her into the inn. He held open the dtior of a low room with heavy beams from one end of the ceiling to the other; suspended from the central beam was a huge rack stuffed with bunches of herbs, skins of lard, smoked pig's flesh, and hams from the wild boar which infested the neighbouring forests. • Jeanne soon found that she was more hungry than she had imagined she could be; but she would not taste the wine which her companion pressed on her. She was impatient for him to finish his meal, and to send away the woman who had served it, and who still lingered, while she looked admiringly at the young Count and his companion. Her women sat at the farther end of the long table, too far off to overhear her talk. At last he bade the wench leave them, and then he turned to Jeanne.

CHAPTER XII.

REASSIIMID. "Now that we are alone, madame, I will answer your question, but I will do so with caution; eavesdroppers may be near Us." He spoke almost in a whisper, and looked over his shoulder as he spoke. Jeanne remembered the hostess's caution, and she wished herself safely out of all this secrecy ans.? mystery, so unlike the fearless atmosphere in whidli she had lived at Longuedec. "Your question implies, dear lady, that the King has reached Lyons; I am not certain that this is the case." Jeanne felt impatient; she said quickly: "That dew not alter my question. My wish is to learn whether the Constable is known to be with the King, or whether he is, like ourselves, riding to overtake his Majeasty ? " Laval looked at her through his half-closed eye- lids; but Jeanne kept a smiling, impassive face. He fancied from her expression that she was merely bent on satisfying a childish curiosity. "It is curious that you, madame, should question me on the point that made me resolve to spend a whole night at Moulins; I also was anxious to learn somewhat of the Duke's movements. I learnt at Moulins that his Highness had continued too much indisposed to set forward with his Majesty, and that when the King quitted the town he left behind him Monsieur Perrot to wait on my lord the Con- stable, who had promised to follow the Royal troops in a litter. Sooner than could have been expected/ I learnt that my lord of Bourbon, Monsieur de Pomperant, and the King's gentleman set forth on horseback, riding swiftly in order to overtake hip Majesty. My informant said the Con- stable's was indeed a marvellous recovery, but then, as madame knows, the old saying has it: 'A King's face giveth grace.' My own opinion is, dear lady, that if they have kept to the road they have by now overtaken King Francis." Jeanne looked thoughtful. Part of this account tallied with the hostess's story; it seemed possible to the girl that Laval was telling her the real facts, and that the hostess had merely repeated idle gossip; even if the Constable had fled away to Chantelles, Laval might not know it. She dis- liked suspicion; she would trust him as long as she could. , "You will not know certainly, then, till you reach Lyons? Will it be long before we get there?" This question also sounded childish, but Jeanne's heart beat rapidly while she waited for his answer. He rose from the table and spoke without looking at her, though he seemed frank. "I am not sure, madame; there will be delay. I mast first go a little out of the direct road in order to leave you in a place of safety; I cannot spare you a strong escort. My duty on reaching Lyons is to seek the. Kin; ; if his Majesty . has not arrived I must wait for him, and learn his Royal intentions respecting you, dear lady, as his ward. I shall then leave my men at the camp, and return with a few trusty fellows to conduct you to your appointed dwelling-place. This is the position." She bowed, and taking her hand he led her out of the house to the mounting-block beside the door of the hostelry, where the horses stood ready. Jeanne felt happier; she rejoiced to find herself' again in the free, fresh air; the dark eating-roor had been musty and evil-smelling. It was also a relief that she need not suspect Laval;' his candour and frankness had impressed her. She felt that she had wronged him by her suspicions • and blushed in thinking that she had credulously listened to the report of an utter stranger, and had actually believed him to be a mercenary spy. Surely she could not have listened so readily but for that childish aversion of so many years ago! "It has been partly Gillonne's fault," she told herself. "She took such a bitter dislike to him at Laval." , So she rode on, happier than she had been since she left Longuedeo ; full of kindly smiles for her companion, to whom she thought she owed repara- tion for her unworthy suspicions of his good faith. She had never been left so entirely to her own devices as she had been in the journey from Laval, and till now distrust of her companion had added to the reticence which she had considered due to her unprotected condition. Now the revulsion of feeling towards him overcame her scruples; she looked at him with sparkling eyes as they cantered on side by side; slie was once more the charming, sprightly Jeanne she had been in the old days at Lon guedec. While,, she chatted and laughed, Laval felt the increasing fascination she had for him; he felt that it would be hard to leave this delightfu,' companion, so suddenly transformed from the prorA: cold girl, who had hitherto accompanied him. The road was long and monotonous till they canto in sight of far-off mountains, some of which had fantastic and quaintly shaped peaks, cutting the line of grey sky. There was no village, only groups of miserable huts. After they tiad passed some of these groups Laval turned to Jeanne: "We shall not find a resting-place where we could pass even a few hours, dear lady, till after nightfall Will it not be better to make a halt beside the road and dine on the provisions we brought from Moulins ? " "That will be excellent," the girl said joyously. "I love journeying, but the inns are nauseous, and they weary me. Why should we not travel through the

H'e laughed at her glad eagerness. "Madame forgets our homes; the poor beasts Will be tired out by the time they are stabled." Then, suddenly dropping his formal manner: "I however, entirely of your opinion—there is nothing to equal the joyous free life of the traveller who can afford to journey only where he pleases and to snatch the golden hours as they fly. When madam° visits Amboise she will talk with the Court . poet; .Messire Clement Marot, who rivals our King ana his sister, Madame d'Alencon, in the art of turning a strophe." Jeanne blushed; she had heard Gillonne say thst ,poets and lute-players and fortune-tellers ought ell to be whipped out of Court together, because of the mischief they made there. But she had begun to see„ that GI-Monne was old-fashioned and full of prejudices. "I am very ignorant," she said. "I am not sure that I have heard the name you meation. I wish to go to Court only for two reasons: I should love to see our gracious Sing, but more than all my own sweet and lovely cousin, the Countess of Chateau' briand." _

Laval told himself that ~.Ml went well Jeanne would be his wife before she had the chance of meeting her cousin;' but he saw that it might stand him in good stead to have special influence with the favourite. "The Countess is, as you say,a truly lovely.lady, and she will know how to make life delightful for you at Amboise. But see, here is a halting-place made for us." He pointed to a little trickling stream that came from a fissure in the rocky banir beside the road, just where the bank itself receded so as to give space to some stunted herbage at its foot.

He helped the girl from her horse, and throwing a cloak on the ground he begged her to be seated*. He waited on her himself with the utmost devotion, and when she inquired for her women he at ono sent for them and placed them near their mistress. Jeanne enjoyed everything; the tiny. lizards even, as they darted in and out of the rocky fissures like so many living emeralds, called forth her delight. When he once more helped her into the saddle Laval whispered : "It will indeed be hard to cut short this happi- ness. Would that our journey might last for weeks!"

She gave him an arch smile. "I cannot agree to that, monsieur iii six weep —perhaps sooner—we may have snow and cold weather; and then there will be no lizards among the rocks, and the butterflies will all be dead." "You speak like a true Northerner," he said, laughing, as they again rode on, some little wel behind the rest of the escort. "But I have Southern blood; my mother came from Provence' where the winter passes over us tenderly, and we have flowers in December." The Bill for distributing electrical power from a lilted centre which has been suspended by special resolution of the House of Commons till next session, instead of being allowed to drop like other tills, is one which calls for the closest scrutiny by Public bodies throughout the country, and by all interested in the administrative arrangements con- nected with local government. If the principle is admitted by Parliament, the Bill viei be a prece- dent for the establishment of a great number of local monopolies 'which, in the Course Of the future tevelopmetit of .electricity as a motive power, must : become of great pecuniary value. The Bill in ques- tion proposes to distribute from a given centre in Nottinghamshire electric energy for the purpose of taiPplyinv light and power within a radius of 30' Utiles. Within tnis radius lie large and populous boroughs and cities, some, if not all, of which already possess powers for supplying electricity- Those) that hare such power will Rod a rival in this .1101npanyithoss that have not may soon find a mono- polist in posseesion with whom it is diffirtult to deal. When such a town, for example, as Sheffield! 411 threatened with invasion by such a company, as it is by this Bill, it is obvious that a network of onopolies may be thrown over the development Of local government, which may be a mtsch more Itrievousduatter than a telephone monopoly. AU local bodies should take steps before next session to 43aft-gllZlrd 'heir freedom and their own finvire 'tights against the monopoly with which a Bill of this kind threatens them.

All this time the reorganisatien of the army Was being pushed rapidly forward, and Bismark was plying his skull as a diplomatist. Ile invited Count Karolyi, the Austrian Ambassador at Berlin, to discuss with him in a confidential conference the position of Prussia and Aus- tria in the German Confederation. In this conference he contended that it would be much better far Austria if she were to transfer her centre of gravity to Pesth in- stead of seeking to plant it in the German Confederation to the exclusion of the legitimate influeace of Prussia,; and he plainly intimated that if his advice were not heeded, Austria would find Prussia in the ranks of her 'enemies at the first opportunity. The conference led to. no result. A momentous event was the death of the King of Den- mark, on the 15th Decombrr, 1863. It opened up the dispute respecting the sovereignty of the duchies of Schleswig-Holstein. Denmark war invaded by the allied Prussian and Austrian troops. The Danish army was overwhelmed and subdued. After the war, the allies ,quarrelled over their spoil—the two duchies of Schleswig and Holstein—and Bismark perceived his chance to humble Austria, and thus solve the problem of supre- macy by the sword. The outbreak of this conflict, in May, 1866, consummated Bismark's unpopularity, so that when, on May the Bth of that year, a young student fired five shots from a revolver at him, without touching him, popular sympathy was more with the would-be assassin than with the intended victim. The war lasted only a few weeks, ending in a crushing defeat of the Austrians. The Prussian Premier, created a count after the war, succeeded in organising the North German Con- federation, comprising 22 States, The King of Prussia was at the head of thefederation. having Count Bismark /as his Chancellor, or Prime Minister. The Legislature I consisted of a Federal Council, in which the sovereigns of the various States were directly represented, and of a common Diet,or Parliament, the members of which were elected by universal suffrage. Count Bismark had suc- ceeded in concluding secret treaties of alliance, offensive and defensive, with Bavaria, Baden, and Wurtemburg. with a proviso that in the event of war the King of Prussia should have the chief military command. The connection with the Southern States of Germany forint d with the new Curare :oration was, it will bi seen only by a loose and unreliable tie, hut it was ere long to be tightened by the " blcod and iron " cement into a close union forming a cempact German Empire. The event which led to this unification was a war with France. It was while the victorious Germans were awaiting the surrender of Paris that the unification of Germany, for which Bismark had been working so effectively during many years, was consummated, by the hailing of King William as Emperor, in the palace of the French sovereigns at Versailles, on January 18th, 1671., The Emperor-King was net unmindful of what was due to his soldier-Minister, who, among other honours conferred upon him by his grateful Sovereign, was raised to the rank of a prince, and appointed Chan- cellor of the new empire. On the conclusion of peace between France and Ger- many,in May, 1871, Prince Bismark devoted himself mainly to the consolidation of the empire, and with that object in view used his influence as the " arbiter of Europe" for the maintenance of peace, taking every opportunity of playing the part of " honest broker" iii the settlement of disputes amongst the Powers. In con- ducting the internal affairs of the empire, the most no- table feature of his policy was his prolonged struggle with the Papacy,during which the Jesuits were expelled from Germany; but on the ',hole the result may be des- cribed as a. 7 drawn battle." Not unfrequently he too cern° into antagonism with party leaders in the Reichs- rath in carrying out measures of home legislation, but from the time his policy for the unification of Germany first bore fruit in the victories won in the Bohemian Campaign till the day of his death, he was beyond all comparison the mcst popular man in the Fatherland. During the lifetime of the Emperor William his power was undisputed. A grateful master paid constant tribute to his devotion, sagacity, ard services. The Sovereign reigned, but the Chancellor governed. In at political movements one hal to reckon with Prince Bis- mark. The aceession to the throne of the present Em- peror, however, inaugurated a new st o, things. His Majesty WAS young, and Remark was there to advise him ; but the Emporer had older views. They did not transpire immediately. He commenced his responsibility by indicating to everybody that he inteuded t be his own Mentor. Thus strained relations came about in a gradual way, and ultimately led to Prince Bismark's re- tirement from office. He was afterwards induced to accept nomination for a Hanoverian constituency, and was returned to the Reichstag ; but his active career was virtually over, not withstaading the popular demon- strations of confidence and affection which were freely offered to him by his countrymen, and all the more heartily when the enthusiasm might be construed as a protest against the treatment he received at the hands of the Emperor. The two principal figures in Germany made their peace not very long age, and the Iron Chan- cellor was then on good terms with all the world.

WARRINGTON F6OTBALL CLUB

ATHLETIC SPORTS.

These sports were held as usual,on Bank Holiday. From all points of view they were a complete suc- cess. The weather was good aßil the attendance probably better than any previous occasion,between six and seven thousand people viewing the pro- ceedings. The surface of the track was in excellent condition but owing to the corners being fl it there was an element of risk about the cycle races. This risk was proved by the frequent spills which occurred, particularly in the novice races. Fortu- nately none of the falls proved serious to the riders, though one or two macoines were wrecked. Of course there is only one remedy for this i.e.a banked I track, which Warrington district is sadly in need of. The result was 8 Brelsford, Stickport, firs: prizs (silver plated tea eel and oak mounted tray) ; E Charnock,Warringtoa,second prize (silver plated six Cup egg frame) ; and W Collin, Warrington, third priz3 (fourteen day marble timeniece).

LOCAL VOLUNTEER INTELLIGENCE

THE WARRINGTON MEN IN MARCHING COLUMN. On Monday eveaing the Warrington Volunteers, or to , be more precise,the Ist V.B. South Lancashire Regiment returned to their headquarters, on the completion of three days' most useful and practical work in the shape of marching and camping under service conditions. This is the first year a marching column has been attempted by the Warrington Battalion,and is,we understind,to be an annual fixture. It isceitainly anether great step in the movement t 'wards rendering the Volunteer force !practical soldiers fit to take the field when called upon to do so,an object which, judging from the test just I applied, the men themselves are most willing to further, and the thanks of the country at large are certainly due ' to the men whe devote their holidays to the work of the nation. " Heavy marching order" was worn through- out,and a very limited camp equipment was taken, orders having been issued that this was to be limited to 401 b. each for officers, Mb- fer non-commissioned officers, and 8113 per man, which was strictly adhered to. Luxuries were therefore, conspicu- ous by their absence. All of the battelion who could attend paradei on Saturday afternaon under the Com- mand of Colonel Ridgway and the following officers : Major Buck,on, Captains Appleton, J. D. Fairclough, B. Fairclough, Captain Adjutant Rooms, and Captain and Quartermaster Hutchinson. Owing to the start being in time afternoon the first day's march was t 3 Whitley Gorse, where camp was pitched on arrival in a field kindly lent for the Occasion, and the evening meal cooked, after which the men "snugged " down for the night. Early next morning the camp was astir, the bugles sounding the reveille at 5.30 a.m., and by 9.30 the hattalionwas again on the march, having had breakfast, struck the tents, and packed the camp impedimenta into the transport waggone in the interim. This time the objective was Vale Royal Perk,and the Cheshire lanes proved hot end duity to the men in their heavy equip- ment in the still air and heat of the morning. In spite of this,hewever, the column marched well and with a good swing, the experience of the previous day showing beneficially in the improved marching. On arriving at Vale Royal a camping ground was kindly indicated by Mr. Jeckson, scent to her I Delamere, who in the and otlesr ways most heartily assist 3d the bat Alien during their stay, and in very quick time camp was again formed and in full swine. :As it was Sunday, the men had an alterneon and ecening t) themselves, many of them atten ring service as Woitegate Church. Monday morning was fidel up in the early part by peradee for instructonel work in came duties. arid es easy spell to prepere for the long march back t I War- rinet Alter mid-day dinner camp was again struck. and the colome start el puncteia'l4 a 2.30 p.mearriving avythWitelerFri—ngat,onwanbaonumt oati,lriateine hour'smn 88 awhtta:li t: at work. As the dust-roeere celumn smile up Bridge- Sof etarreir w'tohr.ksuw'tealiludeliwe.oarkerwnaliik°particulaay noticeable On their return to head varcers, afie: being mended by the Calowl for t .eir good mar :hinc,s and seldierlike behaviour the men Were dismissed to their homes, The march was not enlivened be the Regimen- tal e with the exception of Betidmarer Arm- strong w who mered the whole way, did not a and a who, few dithe idea of marching and playing also. 'tlnpe Besaeanra'i, ntw o who did go, however, did their bast to fill the deficiency, and were heartily cheered be the men for , doing so. The commissariat arrangements were looked after in their usual complet3 and satisfacto.y wee, by the Quartermaster kCeptain Hutchinson) and Quarter-master Sergeant Fetter. Of the men who marched out five only returned invalided in the Regimental ambulance, from causes due to the strain of rapid march with a heavy kit in hot weather, and this is a v.ry ni•olitabie recent THE LYMM VOLUNTEERS AT CUERDEN HALL. THE OLIVER STREET TRAGEDY.

The Warrington convict, James Bate, who was reprieved when under sentence for the murder of his wife in Oliver-street, Warrington, two years ago, was on Saturday visited by his parents, who journeyed to Portland Prison for the purpose.

WARRINGTON TEMPERANCE FEDERATION.

One of the most succesiful of the open air Tem- perance Meetings organized by the Federation, was held on Sunday evening July 31st, at Bridge Foot, under the presiden6y ot Mr. Drury, the speakers being Mr. Patrick O'Brien and Mr. U. Taylor, Police Court Missionary. They had a large and apprecia- tive audience and at the close of the meeting several pledges were taken. WARRINGTON AND BANK QUAY SAILING CLUB. There was a very successful boat race in connec- tion with the above club on Monday, the course being from Widnes, round a buoy moored near Wal- ton Arches, and back to the east end of Thewlis's wail. The boats were handicapped half a minute per foot on length measurement. A good race re- sulted in Mr. S. Leigh's Ernest winning, with Mr. harry Burthem's Not Allowed second, and Mr. R. Warburton's Nellie third. MERTINGS OF SPIRITUALISTS.

On Sunday the platform at the meeting in con- nection with the Warrington Society of Spiritual- ists was occupied by Mr. J. U. Macdonald, of Patti- croft,who in the afternoon delivered an address on " What must I do to be saved ?" and in the evening dealt with questions sent up by the audience in a very able manner. At each meeting the speaker gave clairvoyant delineations. Tnis was Mr. Mac- donald's first Sunday visit to the Warrington so- ciety, and his addresses gave great pleasure to the audiences.

PICNIC TO BUXTON.

The members and teachers of the Bold-street Wesieyen School and Wesley Guild had an enjny- able trip to Buxton on Bank Holiday. The party departed from the Central Station at 8.15 a.m., and after a pleasant ride reached their destination at H a.m. Daring the day they visited Pool's Cavern, and other interesting places, and the outing was greatly enjoyed. Tea was partakeo of at Fleetwood's restaurant, and the party commenced the return journey shortly after 8 p.m. A vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Lunt, the secretary, for making such satisfactory arrangements.

TiE VoTErts' LISTS. These lists are nowpi bliAe,. and can he seen affixed to the notice boards at the churches and chapels,the Liberal Club, and other public places in the town. Liberals will and it to the interests of their party and themselves to examine the lists and see that their names and qualitications are properly de- scribed. In case of error or omission they should at once comoaunicate with Mr. Clayton at the Liberal Club Bewsey-street. All lodgers paying £lO a year and upwards and who are without a vote would also do well to consult Mr. Clayton. Claims for the revising barristers' court to be held in Sep- tember must be made before the 20th inst. WYCLIFFE Y.P.S.C.E. A. lecture was delivered before the members of the Christian Endeavour Society in Wycliffe Hall on Tuesday evening by Miss L. fl. Barnes, from Hang- chow,China. Iler subject was " Missionary work in China," and during her very interesting lecture she exhibited a large number of curios. There was a large audience,and Mrs. rouge took the chair. ' A few words were spoken by the Rev. John Yonge and Mr. George Wallington. A vote of thanks was accorded to Miss Barnes and the meeting termin- ated. TREAT TO THE PADGATE INDUSTRIAL SCHOOL

CHILDREN. The Cheshire Lines Engineers' Club very kindly entertained the children of the Industrial Schools on Saturday. The children, accompanied by the Superintendant, Mr. Wright, assembled on the Cheshire Lines ground in Battersby-lane about two o'clock The first item was a cricket match, the members of the Cheshire Lines club batting.bowling and fielding, left hand, whilst the male children of the school who bad been selected played the ordinary game. During an interval foot races for prizes took place the events numbering 13. The handicapper was Mr. W. Nevins, the starter Mr. Irvine, and the judges Messrs. J. T. Smith, F. Deeley, and W. Kilduff. At the conclusion of the cricket match the children were regaled with buns, &c., and bags of nuts and sweets. The girls of the school could not take part in the proceedings, but they were not forgotten, ample provision being made for their enjoyment at home. Amongst the guardians present on the field were Messrs. Ash- croft and Alfred Jolley, who suitably thanked the members of the club tor their kindness. Mr. Hill responded on behalf of the cricket club.

LOCAL MARRIA.GE.

A large assemblage was present at the Friars' Green Chapel on Tuesday afternoon to witness the nuptials of Miss Elizabeth Bradford Bramhall,seconA daughter of Mr. William Bramhall, of Manchester- road, and Mr. Walter James Birch, of Orford-lane. Both parties having been connected with Friars' Green Chapel for many years. The ceremony was performed by Mr. Thomas Barton,uncle of the bride, The wedding gown consisted of electric blue silk trimmed with white chiffon. The bride carried a handsome bouquet. the gift of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were Miss Bramhall and Mrs. Broom- head (sisters of the bride), and Miss Birch (sister of the bridegroom), who also carried pretty bouquets, the gift of the bridegroom. Mr Harry Birch acted as best man. .jr. Critchley, organist, played the usual selections before and after :the ceremony, whilst as the welding party left the chapel be played Mendelsso , lin's ' Wedding March," Confetti , was showered on the happy couple, and a reception was afterwards held at the Masonic Rooms. Here the wedding breakfast was served, nearly fifty guests being invited. The health of the bride and bridegroom was proposed infelicitous terms by Hr. T. Barton, and in responding Mr. Birch thanked his many friends for the kind expressions which bad been extended to his wife and himself. The guests included Miss Atkinson (lorwich), Mr. and Mrs. Dyson (Bolton), Mr, and Mrs. George Mounfield, Mr and Mrs. John Mounfield, Mrs. Meadows (Staly. bridge), Mr. Mrs., and Miss Barton. Mr. and Mrs. Hutchinson. Miss Whittle, and Mr. W. Whittle. Many wedding pregents were received. The honey- moon is being spent at Morecambe.%

Witr,coces, headmaster, who was heartily received on rising said : Ladies and gentle- men, it has always given me much pleasure to welcome you all here on our annual speech day. The continued interest you evince in these proceedings is a great sup- port and encouragement to the teaching stiff of the school. We are trying to do our duty—if hard work and personal interest in the boys count for anything— and I think that the success attending our efforts dot-

log the past year has not been less than in former years. Our aim here is to give a good " all round " general education, not pushing one or more subjects at the expense of others, still in one branch I have always felt we might be stronger and that is in practical chemis- try, but now that reproach—if it be, a reproach—will be removed, for I have great pleasure in announcing that the governing b dy at taeir last meeting decided to build and equip a chemical laboratory and lecture room, and also to make other improvements in the Iclass-room accommodation which will bring us up to the newest modern requirements. It has hitherte been the want of funds that has hindered these eaten- , slons; the necessity has been recognised for many years. The whole school was examined as usual under the Oxford Local Examination Board,anl fifteen boys in the sixth and fifth forms sat for the certificate. The Interesting feature of the examination re/Illne— ss far we as are concerned—is that all our I • , Candidates satisfied the examiners in Latin and mathematics. Our other successes during the past year include .—H Gibson, head of the school, who has won in open competition a classical exhibition at Queen's College, Cambridge ; J Scott, who has passed the preliminary or marticulation examination of the Victoria University ; J Lamb, the preliminary examina- tion of the Incorporated Law Society ; F Weeks, in the recent examination held by the Civil Service Commis- sioners, for boy clerkships, obtained the 29th place out of 384 candidates ; T L Barnes has won in open compe- tition, a scholarship, to be held at a secondary Ballot, and open to boys for the township of Lowten ; E S Kidd, late head boy, and now a scholar of Gaaville and Caius College,Cambridge, has taken the first place of his year, in classics in the college examinations, and has received an addition of £2O to his scholarship from the college authorities. D Willcocks, of University College, Liverpool, has passed the interme- diate M.B. examination of the Victeria University, and has also been awarded a certificate of honour in surgery; S. Lunt has won an art scholarship of £6O per annum for 3 years, awarded by the Lancashire County Council ; U. 11. Bate I on his appointment to the office of coroner for West Cheshire. Biabant,of Corpus Christi College, Oxf ard, who conducted the examination of the school lase, week :—"To sum up some of the main results I have indicated already taa7, I consider the classical part of the education the backbone of the school. of Liverpool, who at the conclusion of his pleasant duty, delivered an ad Imes. In the course of it he expressed the pleasure it gave him to be present at the invitation of tbe Headmaster to associate himself with the prize distribution. Flo stood before them with pleasure and did fence, hut the difidence disappeared when he realised the character of the prize winners. He WaS pleased to see that the scheol had justified its past records, and maintained its proud position,especially so in these days. As teey were aware there had been called into existence schools aided by government revenue ; and it seemed in the matt( r of secondary education—in the matter of schools:that filled thekap between the elementary school and the university—there was a &sirs for improve. meet. He had only one weri to say to those that had the matter in hand and that was that they would be cooservati vo—conservative to the interests of those who ,in the past had forwarded the work so successfully and well. He sincerely hoped, however, that whatever happened it would not be left in the hands of faddists— and educational faddists at that. He contended that they should not lose sight of individuality, but rather aim for it. Individuality was the power of the indi- vidual, and it was absurd to destroy that power. Ele rejoiced because those schools were a link with the past, they dated back to before the Reformation and showed that there bad been one continuity of effort, and of success, and it was with the utmost sincerity that he congratulated their headmaster upon the situation. (Applause.) To those who had carried off a prize that day, he would remind them that there was a responsibility attached to the privilege. If it was the beginning of the laying of a foundation of future achievement and Success then the responsibility of gaining a prize was a matter of very great congratula- tion. But if it was merely a spurt so te speak, it would be an absolute mischief and an evil. If it was the result of the individual efforts of the prize winner; and not the result of what was beyond their control, well and good. As they knew God had given them differeet kinds of gifts, and some in a greater degree. But their greatness lay in the use they made of tease gifts and not in the gifts themselves, and therefore, if they had only come up to the top without any conscientious effort—it was simply something for which they couldtake no credit.Ele hoped they would go on and make that prize the step- pingstone to greater efforts for SUCCOSS. speaking to those who had been unsuccessful as prize winners that year,be would remind them that true education was the strengthening of the faculties and the storing up of knowledge for future times. Others had the prize—the reward in the struggle they bad started upon, and they should:see it did not stop there. ,•• He was :pleased:to see the number of prizeslthat had been secured for religious instruction. They (the scholars) had solved the religious diliculty—and though someone had said that religious difficulties belonged to the parsons alone, be did not think so, Religious instruction was the best of instruction and they would do well to cultivate it. He requested his hearers ti learn to live goof ; to be in every way those in whom relience could be placed, whether it was a quer tion of word or honour; to be ready to hand over to future generations the rich heritage that had been handed down to them. He ' thanked them for listening to him; congratulated them upon the result, and hoped that God's blessing might shine upon them, even more abundantly than in the I past. (Loud applause.) • The Rev. E. J. Wilcocks then proposed a hearty vote. ; of thanks to the R9V: Canon Tvrer for his kindness in • coming over to distribute the prizes, which was heartily • accorded.

THE PLAYS.

After the distribution of Pi71;;;-, a number of plays were rendered with characteristic clevornr ss by the boys of the school, and were well received. Th..i first "La Grammaire " was a French one, and the dramatis persons as follows :—Francris Caboussat (a retired business man) W B Forshaw ; Poitrirsas (president of the Academy of Erampes), A B wiles ; Afachut (veterinary curgeon), C W Itynolds ; Jean (servant of Cahoussat), F H Cooke ; Blanche (daughter of Caboussat), E Gandy. fhe scene was a room at the house of Cabousaat's, and the plot, though lighr, was suffi iently interesting. A scene from " The Frogs 'of Aristophanes," followed (in the Greek laneu age). and the characters were admir- ably sustained by G E Efesketh, as Dionysus: if Gib- son, Xanthias ; J W Soot, Aeacus ; E A Weeds, Thera- paina Perseplaones.—The concluding piece was a scene from Shakespeare's" Henr s the Fifth" the represmta- Von of which was as follows :—King Henry the Fifth, W D Macrae ; Pistol,J E Bryant ; Fluellan. G if ,p,s,,ph ;French soldier, G T Burke ; Gower, CT Cooke: boy, 11 A Holford.—The plays were well staged. and well did the actors sustain their various parts ; and the objsct of it. to show the thorough tuition of the scholars in the several languages, was doubly aahieved. The enunciation and nronoanciation showed that the training received had b •en of the highest order.—Dur- ing the intervals between the various pieces, the boys rendered a varied programme of songs, with telling effect.—At the conclusion cheers wero given by the bey, for the ladies, the governors, and thv masters, and also . .

for themselves. The whole concludin% With the " National Anthem." •

The wigs and make-up were by Mr Bertram Brown, nf Sankey.strect, and the costumes were supplied by Mr. Buricirishaw, of Liverpool. The stage was elaborately decorated with plants,and flags and bunting were freely suspended from all parts of the roof, adding greatly to the gaiety of the scene.

BANK HOLIDAY AT WAItUINGTON.

The weathertwhich on brdinary days is but a mere platitude and serves to fill up many a gap in a con- versation,is a serious consideration to all of us on a Bank Holiday. However on Monday all doubts as to the weather not being fine were early dispelled by tbe bright appearance of the sky and later in the day the weather developed charmingly. Numbers of people made their way to the villages of interest in the neighbourhood,and a very considerable num- ber left town for Liverpool,Manchester, Soutlfport, Blackpool and Rhyl. Of course the devotees of cycling contributed their share to the holiday makers. Many people were attracted from outside towns to the Football Club Sports at Wilderspool in the af,prnoon, and in the evening the Theatres and Parr Hall were extremely well attended.

THE occurrence of two or three The Grimsby cornered contests within a month Election. is a remarkable phenomenon, for on the surface it would seem to indicate that we are in process of dividing up our already much divided parties into more minute sub- divisions. But this would be a most erroneous conclusion to draw from the contest at Reading and the contest at Grimsby. The divisions may rmosthe described as purely local. That at Read- ." certainly was. The third party never had any I,"allee,and it would hardly have madea stir at all -Ut for certain local allegations. Tae Grithisby cOntest had some local causes, but they were more 'fixed with general features. The contest was nught about by a change of opinion, and it was xollowed by another change in the locality itself, la the form of a Conservative Secession, the strength of which it was impissible to estimate lintil Tuesday's poll came wita its ravelaHoas. lii 'various constituencies there is rather a gro jug tendency to regard the Liberal Unionists as a minority which could not exist were it not for Conservative self-denying support, and Mr. MELnuisEr's candidature gave expression to this view, not unmixed with several other con- aiderations, difficult to appraise at their proper ''alno outside the constituency itself, and even by L°rae of the best experts who have been sent to tirvimehy to enlighten the readers of their respec- e inurnals. We can now see that the strength of this rebellion has been exaggerated, Mr. MEL- "utast( only polling a little less that Mr. QUELCH, the third man, polled at Reading. Mr. BALFOUR'S letter had, doubtless, considerable counteracting effect. Apart from this third man bogey, which haslbeen so completely knocked on the head, the result is a remarkable testimony to the strength Of Mr. DOUENITY'S position and popularity. He has held the position he had acquired, but with a change in the votes to which he owes it. He has lest some 3,000 odd Liberal votes, but he has car- ried with him quite as many Liberal Unionists and Conservatives,and probably the difference required to make up his total poll may be regarded as repro-. seating personal and social frieads. The result is a surprise, and yet it bears some remarkable re- semblance to what took place at Reading. Mr. DOUGHTY will go back to the House of Cana- Mons in time to take part in the close of the session, and to discover, perhaps, that if the finance of Nationalism in Ireland is peculiar, so Is the finance of Colonialism in the West Indies. For the rest, it may be said that more attention has been given to Mr. DOUGHTY and to Grimsby than either the member or the borough deserve. Circumstances have compelled it, and they will .11,40 compel a contest another time, when Mr. ucarry may have the fate of the wobbling mem- ber who proceded him, and was finally ej so ted 11:!:1121 the representation by no 1033 a person than -Kr. DOUGHTY himself. Lovers of consistency will regret than a sudden perversion has been condoned by a wobbling constituency,but that will probably not be unmixed with praise of Mr. Wirrrairramtm and the gallant fight he has made, and the hope that he may be available and successful another time.

THE German Empire The Garman Empire— will it Last. is generally spoken of as if Prince BISMARCK had left it solid and permanent, superior to external at- tack and internal disintegration. Is it so ? The question must be asked by Germans no less than by other people. It was built up by force, and by force it will have to be maintained. That force is military, and the demands made upon the People for service with the colours, for the subor- dination of commerce to militarism, and for enormous, never-ceasing expenditure are calcula- ted to suggest doubts as to its permanence. Ex- ternally, there is always France, and now there is Russia, by no means as friendly as in former times and suffering from the effects of BISMARCK'S Policy. The establishment of the German Empire 'was welcomed in this country as relieving Europe from. the preponderating power of Russia. But Russia has been growing stronger at a tremendous rate, in armed men, in strategic railways,. le general resources. What Germany was in the late Prince BISMARCK'S hey-day of power, that Russia is now. Austria, moreover, is weaker now than she has been at any time since Sadowa. The Triple Alliance is a poor skeleton of a thing to What it was. The alliance between Russia and 11:rance assures the latter from German aggression, if it does nothing else. France, on the other hand. Will not attack Germany without the assent of Russia, and when that assent is given the German Empire will be in evtremis. The fear is that Austria may break up, and that its German pro- vinces may be added te the German Empire. This Would narrow the frontier of Russia to the south- West, and probably bring about friction between Russia and Germany. These are the external dangers; but they are nothing as com- fared with the internal perils to which Germany exposed. As Prince Bismancic was well Ware, he was not loved by anybody. He made an empire, but it was at a terrible price. He des- troyed political parties, he interfered with the

constitution, and he created Socialism, which neither his laws nor his repressions have dimin- ished. To-day the German people hardly love the •mpire, and it has done nothing to demand their affection. It has reduced their liberty and in- creased their taxes. Emigration to the United States is the great evidence of the lack of affection I for existing conditions. The common people work hard and fare hard. Waat have they in return F. The glory of empire! It hardly seems to be real and satisfying, and to present them with objects for venetation. They were better off in the old days. But they would probably forget their yearn- ings for more equal conditions if the empire were really attacked from without, and they would be compelled to fight by the iron discipline which is part of their Imperial system. Left alone, with an ever-increasing burden of empire, they may be- come revolutionary ; and probably no one has keenerfears as to this evantually than the E,nperor whose mistakes may grow more fatal as he grows older.

IT is always safe to assume tha liWuasubtd A every business man knows his trade better than does an outsider, but on appearances the most casual observer would think that the matrimonial agents were somewhat lack- ing in discretion. They have a way, in the col- umns of the weekly papers, of raising doubt by inverting the order of human experience. The mind is too accustomed to the hard, matter-of- fact, commonPlac sordid character of human e, existence to credit that a banker's daughter, young and beautiful with large capital, should have to appeal to the people because she wishes to marry. Could not the young man of stereo- typed fiction—the banker's clerk, with small pay, Lut of geatle birth—oblige ? Then, too there is that other young lady who desires to me-el with a husband to invest her capital of £12,000. simple that a sophisticated mind is inclined It is so simple at it, and it is only the sophisti- cated who need to seek a wife in such a column. Of course no one can say that there are not private advertisements, but either way it is a pity that any lady with such ample means should not have a few more peace to break the news to srepaadreci ers. Such a brief statement conveys a dark hint of miserliness. It is a point the matrimonial agent should remember. Ode advertiser, i e is interesting to note, has improved on the usual unblushing avowal of loveliness. " Considered good-looking" has a trace of the feminine equivalent for modesty. It will no doubt be copied. Bat it would be still better to throw in a blemish to make the creature human. After all, however, though one may smile at the insiacerity of these appeals to Cupid, be they private or inspired by a profes- sional adviser, they are few and tame compared with the long list of love-lorn maidens and men who figure in Mr. STEAD'S new hymeneal maga- zine. These are credited by the eeLtor as genuine and they suggest that young people are growing hard to please. In some cases a baleful influence of heredity may be visiting the sins of the parents upon the children, or it may be the parents them- selves may be the deterrent. It has been put for- ward that the isolation of country life causes these calls in the open. But for young ladies with means there is always the resource of the board- ing-house, and the personally-conducted tours, in which anyone may play, even in England, the part of a "summer girl," dropping the acquaint- anceship whenever convenient.

Actors and play-wrights are beginning to turn on their critics in a retaliatory and rather un- pleasant spirit. They have taken to prosecuting for libel the writer who dares to say anything against either the play produced by them or their interpretation of the parts, A striking instance in point was afforded at the Liverpool Assizes on Tuesday, when Mr. Edmund Tearle sued the "Birmingham Daily Post" for libel contained in a criticism of a play written by his wife and pro- duced by hinvelf, entitled "The Christian's Cross and the Martyr's Crown." Briefly stated, the case was this. The dramatic critic of the " Post " attended the play in question, was unfavourably impressed with it, and gave the public his un- varnished opinion. We must admit that his con- demnation was a rather sweeping one, and likely to touch the tender susceptibilities of the author. But that is not the point at issue. The criticism was a plain and unbiased expression of opinion from a person whose duty and privilege it was to publicly express the opiniou. Nevertheless the jury awarded the plaintiff 210 damages, and in so doing, we are at liberty to presume, decided that the criticism exceeded the bounds of fairness. Now the problem arises, what is fair criticism ? We certainly cannot answer the question from a legal point of view, though we have our own firm opinion. Again, are actors to be protected—even exempted from criticism? If so it will be an equally bad thing for the stage and the people. Mr. Justice Bruce stated clearly that everyone in a public capacity, including actors, was liable to be criticised. Bat if the criticism can only be exercised at the expense of an action for libel with damages against the critic, then it is not worth much. It is to be deplored that the law does not exact a statement from the jury of their reasons for finding a ver- dict Then there would be some guide as to what is admissible and what is inadmissible.

According to report, gold is not the only valu- able thing to be got at Ktondyke. Mr. Roderick Dhu Smith, of San Francisco, asserts that not only fresh air, bat fresh hair can be had without asking in Alaska. Two years ago he took with him to the new Eldorado a bald and shiny pate, and has now returned with a head of hair" like that of a Circassian girl." Not having seen a Circassian girl in these parts, we are left to imagine what the head of hair was really like. But apart from this, the fact remains that Mr. Roderick Dhu Smith—of what clan P—went to the north and hairless and came back with a most satisfactory crop. Undoubtedly it is remarkable. We have heard of men going to regions adjacent to Kton- dyke with as much hair as ever they had, and coming back with none, not even the scalp. The cause and effect in the two cases differed great:y however. The extreme coldness of the climate in Alaska is advanced as a reason for this sudden profusion of hair on the head of the erstwhile hairless, and another rush to Klondyke is naturally to be anticipated now that the famous region affords the double attraction of nuggets and a cure for baldness innocent of quackery.

The human document which appears in one of the weeklies above the name of Rpsslyn needs to be read in the light of the application for discharge which was heard this week in the London Bank- ruptcy Court. It would be a mistake to get up any sympathy for an Earl who, with heal th,a good name, and plenty of intelligence found £15,000 a year inadequate for his tastes. It is true that in later years this representative of a noble house cut down his expenditure to between £3,000 and £4,000 a year, and that he is at last trying the problem of working for his own living. The most interesting fact he relates in his experiences is that he finds work the best antidote for depression. As an actor and magizine editor, the tune which in- variably hung on his hands if he were not speeding Imoney he now finds to pass most agreeably in earning it. He tells us that he suffers a great deal from anxiety and mental anguish, such as those who toil not, neither spin, do not know; but these are incidents in everyone's life who has to make his own living. The Earl has apparently , learnt a great deal more in the last year than he

did in his days of proligality, and there is some reason to hope that when he returns to the House of Lards he may be able to bring to that place a knowledge of social struggles that few of those in it now possess. He will also have learnt from his practice in the dramatic art how to make himself heard—a very necessary accomplishment in the Gilded Chamber.

If the report presented by two clergymen to the Whitechapel Guardians regard:ng the burial of paupers be correct the people have a most serious grievance. It is stated by these gentlemen that when they visited the canetery they found that only in three cases out of fourteen were the bodies removed from the hearse to the chapel. The rela- tives and friends preferred to remain with their ' dead outside the church to going into the church without them, and the consequence was that the ' service was a mockery. Still worse, it is said that in some cases the mourners followed the wrong bybodies through not hearing the announcement y the clerk, though he does not appear to have been to blame. This haphazard, cursory disposal of the dead in the cemeteries in the East End shows that Tom Hood's lines about the pauper's burial are as true to-day as is, unhappily, his description of the seamstress's lot. There is a fine saying to the effect that our ballads are more impirtant than our laws, inasmuch as they influence the sentiment that makes those laws. But this does not work out in practice, and it would seem indeed, that familiarity with social evils makes men who might produce alterations callous to them. Wii en one thinks too, how dead poor are hustled into their graves any sensitive person must shudder to con- template how their last days among the living may be spent.

AN UNHAPPY WIFE'S END.

On Saturday morning. Mr. I.E. Worsley, coroner,held an inquest touching the death of a woman named Bridget MacD nmett, wife of John MacDernett, 1, Ash- worth's-row, Runcorn, which took place early on Friday morning. Mr. De Korte was the foreman of the jury, and the police were represented by P.C. Gregory. John MacDornett, chemical labourer, gave evidence of identification. Dsceased. who was his wife, died on Friday morning at abut 2 30. She had been ill for three weeks during which time she was attended by Da Robinson. The doctor had not seen her for the last fort- night. Witness asked her on Thursday if she requlrrd , anything,to w. ich sh 3 replied she did not require either a doctor or the police. She complained that day of a pain in the chest. The pain was severe for a few minutes. Altogether thei deceased was in bed about a fortnight. When he offered to go for a doctor she for- bade him. For food she usually took a cup of tea and an egg. He had seen the doctor in the Square and could easily have cadsd him in if his wife would have allowed him to. She was a g iocl living woman and a total abstainer. About five or ten minutes before she died deceased took some cider. Ho (witness) was lying on the gore downstairs when his wife shouted for him. The time would be about 2 o'clock. The deceased had got out of bed,and died before he could get her back again. He then called in a neighbour. His wife was f nsured,the premium being twopencea week. Dr. Robinson said he had known the deceaseul about six weeks. She consulted him for an ailment that was not at all likely to terminate fatally. He believed she was a very good Woman, and had been a total abstainer for a number of years. She wae respectable but had disagreements with her husband. Witness knew of nothing to which he could attribute cause of death, bat was of opinion that death was due to natural caus3s accelerated by pain and anxiety consequent on her hus- band's conduct towards her. If the man had seat for witness, he mh,ht have averted the fatal consequences. Police-Constable Gregory stated tha3 there Was a young woman from Liverpool staying at the house of the , deceased Some short time ago. In summing up, the Coroner observed that the deceasod had led a most exemplary life. She was a Woman much advanced in yeirs, and had a husband much younger than herself. Dr. Robinson had said that the woman had died from natural causes, aggravate I by her mental trouble, and be thought the husband wag very much to b.a ne. He was to blame for his moral character, which would be a matter far his conscience ; and, independent of what the woman may have s rid, his was to blame because he did not of his own free will call in medical awistanle. There could b 3 no goestion but that it interested tie man not to get a doctor, and prob- ably he allowed his int Test to carry him away. The jury returned a verdict in accordance with the medical evidene,,, and expressed the Opinion that the man should he severely censured. The Coroner accord- ingly severely reprimanded the man for his conduct towards his wife.

ALDINCI FaTaLICY at HU /COWL --DEaTil OF a LITTLE Boy.

Oa Tuesday, at the Police Court, Runcorn, Mr. J. E Worsley,coroner, held an inquest on the body or Cha.dea Gaytor, 3 years old son of Mr. John Glyter, Spring Gar- dens, Runcorn, who died on Saturday from the eff Tts of maids re :eived on Friday, jOl7 22nd.
