THE ALKALI LITIGATION. JUDGMENT FOR THE OASTNER KELLNER COMPANY.

(Before Ma. JUSTICII BMW& sitting as an additional Judge of the Chancery Division.) Judgment was delvered on Thursday in the Chancery Court in this acton, the Castner Kellner Alkali Company v. the Commereial Development Corporation (Limited). the hearing of which was begun on July 13 and occupied six days. Mr. net:her Moulton QC., Mr. Bluefield. Q.C., Mr. J. Graham. Mr. A. I. Walter, and lend Hebert Cecil appeared for the plaintiffs ; and Mr. T. Terrell, QC., Mr. Astbury, Q C., Mr. MscConkey, and Me. J. H. Grey for the defendants.. ban. Jimmie BIGHt.Iff delivered juigment as follows: —lbis is an aceson brought for are intringerneut and to resirain further threatened iafringements of letters potent, No. 20,25) of 1894, for al invention of inepreve- meets in electreiytic apparatus for decomposing metallic sots. The infringement is said to consist of the use of certain apparatus reads aaeording to the specification, and other let'.e.s.patent, No. 21,509,0 f 1896, belonging to the defendants. The defences are practically two. First, the defers lams sty that the plaintiffs' patent is bad for disconform ty ; and, secondly, deny the infringement. Both the apparatus ot the plaintiffs and that of the defendants are designed for use in the manufacture of an article of commerce known as caustic soda. It is common ground that caustic a eles is produced by a process which may be shortly described as follows : A solution of common salt in water is subjected to an electric current while in coevals with mercury, the effect being that the sodium in solution is precipit seed into the mercury and formsan am eleam, The chlorine, which is also contained in Me solution is, by the same operation driven off in the form of a gas. The amalgam of mercury and sodium is then subjected to the opera- tion of °Min try water; the chemicel effect is that the , sodium in the amalgam leaves the amalgam and com- bines with tee oxygen in the water, brining oxide of sodium, ordinarily called soda. The water thus impreg- nated is then evaporated or otherwise dealt with, the Iproduct resulting being caustic soda. The chamber in I which the amaleam is produced is called the" decona- I posing chamber," that in which tee sodium is extracted from the amalgam is called the "combining chamber." „Thus the mercury is charged with and is discharged of the sodium, and the operation Can be repeated with tee same body of mercury for an iudefloite number of times. Tins method of producing caustic soda has been matter of common knowledpe for a very long time, and is not the subjezt of any patent. Th 3 plaintiffs' and the defendants' patents relate exclusively to the apparatus for carrying out the process. It is to be observed that the plaintiffs' patent (known as ' Kellner's) had never been put to practical use, the plaintiffs employing other apparatus which they think Ipreferable. So also with the defendants' patent (known as Ithodin's). Many apparatus have been from time to time devised for carrying out the process which I have described. One of them which was frequently referred , to during the trial was known as the Castner apperatus, land was the subject of a patent taken out in 1832 Tide lapparatus provided a means of producing caustic soda by a continuous process carried on by means lof a quantity of mercury moving round land round in its containing vessel. There had been other apparatus invented for producing caustic soda, and in all of t lem, as I understand, a meviug holy of M9fell.y—that is to say, a body of mercury snoring !el ttivelv to its containing vessel—was an eesential characteristic.. In 1894 Dr. Kellner. of Vienea, toek out the patent which the plaintiffs now own and wnich they aelege the defendants have infringed. Dr. Kellner in his spec.ficatiens describee his inveutin3 as al appiratus for the els ctrolyes of alkaline salt wit's tee ad of a st etionary mercury CA Pole. Instead of having recouree to a mercury cathode which is moved by paddles or otherwise relatively to its containing ves- sel, he provides a stationary mercury cathede—t tat is to ray, he provilee a body of mercury venial is always stati scary ia its centaiaiag vessel ; it does not m ive rued, nor does it m eve b sckwards or for wares in its centaioing vessel. He thus dispens's with the peddles or other machinery for pa; tine the mercury .n melon and is able to conduct tee operation of electrolysis. with a less qu entity of mer- cury than is required in the Cestner process. 'lt was ar .eied on behalf of the defendants that this elven:don was bad for want of subjsct matter and because it was of no utility. But I think,having regard I to the known processes at the date of the patent, and to the character of the chanees in the apoaratue made by Dr. Kellner, the invention was new and a efficiently ingenious to form the subject matter of a grant of letters patent, and, although possibly the apparatus was not capable of producing the best resuite in comparison with other apparatus, I think it offered a useful alterna- tive method, and, therefore, compiled with all the necessary requirements as to utility. Now, after de- scribing the nature of his inventioa in his pro-- visional specification, Dr. Kellner proceeded by means of five diagrams to show how the apparatus might be constructed and worked. The defendants pointed out truly enough that the app sta- tus described in these diagrams contemplated only a te and fro movement of the bell or line of bells over the surface of ehe mercury, and they thee turned to the complete epecification and insisted that the latter de- puted from thoe provisional speoifieation in material particelars whico involved a fatal disconformity, !al. much as they entirely destroyed the notion of a station- ary mercury cathode which was the essential part of the patented apparatus. It is necessary to remember haw the apples us described in the previsional specifica- tion works. The bell which encloses or forms the de- coniposing chamber, area the open bottom of which is sealed by the mercury,le made to move to and foe, an], in order that it may not carry the mercury with it as it so moves, openings in the shape of notches are made in the bottom edge of the bell, or, if not, small sluicee are made on the bottom of the vessel on whieh tea boll rests. Thus the bell moves backwards and forwards without moving the inercury, and the mercury which is alternately under the bell and outside the bell is Wens in turn charged with and discharged of the sodium. N ow, turning to the complete specification, 'we fin i four additional diagrams numbered 6,7, 8 and 9'. Figure 6is describ din the complete specification as a modified construction of the apparatus shown in figure 2. It involves merely a change in the contents of the bsll and the nec a ary alterations oecteinned by he change. The bell isnow made to hold toe water in toed of the solution of salt, an I this b 'corn es a combining , chamber instead of a decomposing chamber. The mre miry cathode remains stationary as before, and the bell or line of be Is moves to and fro as mieinelly described. This modification in my opinion involves no departure from toe previsional specification. But it is upon the change introduced by ligurcs 7 and 8 that the defen- delta mainly rely in support of their de eine of discon- forrnity. These figures are described as "a funher modified con•truction of .the said apparatus "—viz., the apparatus which had been prot -cted by the pro- v;sional specific ition, and the main question in this , action—indeed, I am not sure that it is not in reality the I I only question—is whether they are merely a modified ' conetructian of or whether they ate som:tning which Involves a depar sere from th provisional specificaion. Now, I have to consider what the expression" station- ary mercury cathode "iii the provisional specification means. I think I reading it as any one acquainted with the knowledge then possessed woaid rests it—l mean acquainted with the fact that up to 1894 a flowing body of mercury flowing in its vessel as milk moves in a pan when it is stirred had been always in use—that what Dr. Kellner , meant and conveyed was that the mercury was to be ' stationary with reference to its containing vessel. Perhaps the market was a declining qaautity,but he had not heard a word to prove that it was not paying. Mr. Collier : It is not paying as a market. I In the " Wide World " Magazine for August, is the ,Icommencement of the story of the advcutures of M I Louis de Rougemont, the " mcst marvellous man in the world." At an early age M. de Rougernont left his home in Switzerland to seek his fortune in the French possessions in the Far East, but metting with a Dutch warier at Singapore he threw in Lis lot with him, and after the necessary regotiatiens were completed young De Rougemont set out on a pearling expedition in the South Seas. After about ten months work a large for- but owing to tha fact that Dttranße oiunopmeaornlst wdiasscoaymearegedid, n one shell three black pearls of wondrous beauty, his paltaer, the Datchman,insist-d upon continuing the search, although the time was upon them when the monsoons were due to change. One day when the Dutchman and his Malays had gem oat pearl- ing, De Rougemont and a dog being left alone on board the schooner, a great storm arose, and the young Frenchman never again saw his comrades. For many days he navigated the ship single-handed, but was at length east upona desert island—ant a gorgaaus.tropical place, but a tiny spit of sand me isuring about 100 ards in length. 10 yards in width, 8 feet above high-water mark. In this ghastly and appalling prison he spent two and a half years, and the story of bow he managed to keep his reason during these terrible months must be read to be adequately realized. Eventually M. de Rodgemon reached the mainland of Australia (M.N. W. coast) in a very remarkable manner, and from that time commen- ced a se.ies of adventures more weird, more horrible, more appalling, and more astounclinie than any ever conceived in the wildest fizlits of the novelist's im igination. Fr nearly thirty years M. de Rougnmont was a cannibal chief, ruling his people With the wisdom of the serpent, and maintaining he autho- rity over them in ways that are an astoun iirig revel 'don of human ingenuity. It is not to) much to say that the ' narrative of "The Adventures of Louis de Rouge nont " will create a pro'oand sensation thr ughout the civlized world, the first long and thrilling instalment appears in the current number of " The Wide Would." M. del Roulimunt comes in daily to tel his rtory, which is taken down verbatim from his lipson I iltistr‘td under his own dLect supervision. The narrative, eirsggerat-ci as the s:atement may +we +T. is far more thrilling ard rernirkable than the two cla,s cc. T.V.si;itsoFrr byuYtheiwllyin;l°lans" already hen RoVusoa Crueoe," beon gone into by such eminent geographical experts as Dr. Scott Keltis and Dr. Hugh Robert Mill, who are perfectly natistiocl as Ito its correctness, and have thoroughly checked it by means of charts, latest explorers reports, etc., etc. The scientificconsid considered ds ibd yh e taloneese of experts rdtes o f tf osuugee h g m onrte' sa t narrative is oCfonszcireezess o, fwtillec Britishw illbeAssociation i for the imp r t a n c e that a paper is being prepared to be read at the British advancement n September.

ANCIENT AND MODERN BRITONS.

" Tha anc'eat Britons," says Plutarch, " only began o grow old at 120 years." Th-iy were a wonderful people—.;hose ancient Brito-,.. Authorities say they were remarkable for tbeir fine athletic form, their great strength and swiftness of foot, They excelled in wr s hog, climbing, mad all sorts of p tvcd eaetc:se. 'lThey wee patimt of pain, toil and suffe•ing, and accustomei t t ear hunter, cold, and al manner of tia.-Is,iips. They coeld rim into morasses whereof EaLland;had Vent? in Z.C(Fe time—and lived th,re for days withoat eating,up to their necks in inavk and mire. The'r arm., eg..4, and thighs were always left nak‘el, and. fr che in etport, were pair.te I blue— a neat, cheap, a if attr active style of deco ation. And thPy could fight too—as we might imagine. So aim Romans disc.wered when, in their masterful war they undertook to get possession of this island. Gold- smith ears the Ancient Britons lived almost wholly on scorn, berries, arid water, a diet which the poorest of Modern itritani+ would regard AR v ry bin and nneatis. factory. Y-t they were splendid speciinnna of man- hood, and didn't begin even to think about growing old until they had enj)yed 120 years of active

That the modern Briton falls below this standard will not he disputed. You may remind me that the ancient Brion WV,' a savage. Well, what does that prove ? It proV?s that a savage life is more conthnive to health than a civilized life, Why ? It proves that he modern Briton is phys,cally a degener- ate. Again, why ? While you are turning the matter over in your mind I will copy a short chapter from the autobiography cf a modern Briton.

" For ovar fourteen years," be writes, •‘ I was ill. M,-st of the time I had a poor appetite, and after eating suffered from weight and distention at the chit and at the stomach a grinding, gnawing pain. Some- times it seemed to me that my intestines wi ra tied in a knot. Exhausted, tired, and more or !less in pain, I live] ale ng thus yea alter year. "Ira alsrah, 294, I took a chill, which was followed by an attack of rheumatism. I had dreadful pains all o r me, asd my hands aisd feet were stiff and drawn. F inails' I w s obliged to crawl to bed on my hand 3 and knees• Walk I cost I flit, a, y more than I could fly. All my chest and left ad . was affected, and I was in ako lies of pail. I was 'mai la to turn in bed or to take my arm, out of the he 'clothes. A:doctor attended M 9 for ei ht w. eks and prescrio. d fo.nentatiims, hich g ye bat little relief. 0 Whit mday (UN), through a letter of recom- mendation frorn,Rev. Canon Dyke, Vicar of:Ail Saints. I. was removed ia a eab to the Maidstone Hospital. TIV2IB I lay in bed five weaks, living solely on milk. Two wells later lIA s the hospital,feeling a trifla batter. But I was a low-sairited, dejecel man, with an idea that I should never be of much use again.

" In August my friend, Mr. 1::-mt. of tii:s pi,ace,urged me to try Mother Seigel's Syrup, which, he said, cured him. I had no faith iu it, but got a bottle from Messrs. lorverett and Frye, in High-etreet , and after using it a few days felt much rolieved. Continuing to take this rnedicini tho p tin and stiffaess rapidly abate I, and after consult.): ng four bottles I was completely well and have not had a pain or an ache since. " The pain and dietre=s after meals alio dis Appeared, ' and I am now so bright and light hearted I wonder if I am the came man. My friends were astonished at My recovery, and I tell:them, and everybody, that it was entirely due to Mother Seigel's Syrup. I will gladly reply to incoiries."—(Signed) Maurice Goodield, 78 Melvelle-road, Maidstone,Kent, Jancary 30, 1895. To discuss the general influence of a civilised life on the health and physical structure of man is impossible in this bri f a rticle. One thing, however, i 3 certain, that civilisation, as now underatood, carries with it condi- tions which impair or cripple the most important funC- time of the body—namely, the digestion. This is the prevailing disease of recent times and toe prolific mother of nearly all tho ailments which afflict us —mak- ing pain and weakeeis universal, spoiling our enjoy meat and our working power, and sending us to our graves year before we should be due there under other circumstances.

Abolish this curse and 1 sie no reason why the modern Briton should not improve until a comparison with his ancestors did him less discredit than (we must admit) it dos at present. And so far as a single remedy can con- tribute towards this result Mother Seigel's Syrup does its part. And what a man for business, adventure, dip. lornaey, and war, the average Brion will hi, when his life is a stream of pewer, unbroken by illness and un- touched by time for 120 years. We smile grimly at the fond ; yet, mingled with the soil of England is the dust or men to whom such an experience was the rule. Why may it not be so a_gain ? Why not ?

A remarkable accident occurred on Tuesday morning ten miles oftYarnoouth. on the torpedo-destroyer Porcupine. to Seaman Thomas Mitchell. belonging to Plymouth. A tor- pedo had just been discharged, when its propellor caught Mitchell's wrist, and the blades, making 1,21.0 revolutions per minute, cut through his wrist, severing the bone and tendons, and causing profuse hernorrage.

A number of worknaen engaged in alterations behind Maj,r House, Clitheroe, have discovered in the course of the excavation the skeleton of a human body. Prince Bismarck died on Saturday night. His condition, which had been regardeo as favourable on Friday. changed for the worse on Saturday, and at eleven o'clock after being unconscious for some hours, he passed peacefully away, sur- rounded by his Wally. The news was received with some- thing like consternation in Berlin. The Emperor was on the Net wegian ccast in his yacht. He was profoundly effected by the news of the death of the great statesman,and gave otders at once for the return to Kiel.

On Friday night three boys were playing on the banks of Rockdale Canal near the Ten Acres Lane Lock, Newton Heath. Their attention was attracted by something in the water, and the eldest of the three, Leonard Parkes, eight years of age, residing in Belgrave-street, fell into the lock. His ceropanions ran home to their parents but before assistance was at hand the boy had disappeared. The buoy was afterwards recovered.

The oldest Watt engine in the world is said to be one in the possession of the Birmingham Canal Navigation Co. It was canstructed in 1777 by Messrs. Bonito,' and Watt, an i was built with a cylinder of 321 n. diameter and a stroke of Sit. It has been in regniar work since its erection till the beginning of the present year. when it was removed to the company's station at Ocker Hill, Tipton, where it will be re-ere 'ted and kept as a relict. Blectrlc aerial beacons for lighting city streets may not improbably be brought into use before long. The Russian military authorities have b en experimenting with captive balloons carrying powerful search lights, and the effcct ot a single cluster of 5,00-candle power raised 1,807 feet high is to give thorough illumination over an area below of 500 yards diameter. The cable through which electricity is supplied to the lamp forms a core to the anchoring rope. Mr. Thomas Kibon. a woollen mill owner, of Stroucisberg. Pennsylvania. at one p.m. on May 18th donned a suit of clothes made from a fleece which was on the sheep's back at seven o clock in the morning of the same day. This instance ot lightning shearing, spinning, weaving. cutting. and sew- ing is stated to mark a record. The next to it was a former championship held by Gatashiels,Seotland, where eight hours were taken in gaing through a similar performance.

An inquest was held at Chadderton, near •Oldham, on Wednesday, on the bodies of William Shaw, labourer, ayed forty-Ilya, and Elizabeth Taylor, aged twenty one, who died, it is supposed, from the effects of eating veal pies on Satur- day last. Over twenty persons who partook of the pies are suffering from the effeeta,and the jury adjourned the inquiry for a wok ia order that a post-murtem examination should be made.

A coroner's investigation was held at Ryde on Tuesday into the death of Ernest Veseey by drowning on Bank Holiday. Deceased in the evening went to the pier and fell asleep on a Belt. At ten o'clock he was seen to be hanging over the side of the pier by the rails, and next instant he fell Into the water. His body was not recovered until Tuesday morning.—There was nothing to suggest s uicide, and the jury returned an open verdict. A. terrible yachting accident occurred four m Iles north of Douglas on Tuesday. Five visitors to the island—two of whom were women—had gone out mackerel fishing with Mr. EWMIS, of Liverpool, in his yacht Katie. The yacht capsized off olayhead,and Mr. Evans and all the members of hls party but one were drowned. The survivor was Miss Bates, daugh- ter of a professional swimmer. She was able to swim to a rick, and afterwards, with much difficulty, to make her way ashore. One of the men also swam to the rocks, but was washed off. Several of the visitors belonged to Liverpool. The disadvantage of the dynamite gun,used with such effect by the Americans lately, lies in the comparatively short limit of its range. It is. however, proposed to remedy this defect, and the method of doing so bids fair to admit of such extension that no bounds can be net upon possible theoretical performances. Along the tube through which the charge of dynamite is driven by the force of expanding air a series of supplementary apertures are to be provided, and as the shot passes each of these a further blast of air from a compression chamber will be admittel. Given a sufficiently long tabs and a sufficient number of apertures, and the projectile eon be thrown for miles.

The great model of the earth, which has been projected and talked of with Increasing frequency of late, is apparently nearing the stage of materialism. M. Realm', the French scientist, has prepared particulars of a plan for constructing a ball whose surface will represent by each square luck a model of 64 square miles of the earth. The depth of every river and the light of every bill of importance will bo repro- duced to scale. The ball will be 84ft in diameter, or about half the size of the dome a St. Paul's. The nistanee separat- ing New York and London on the eutface will be 30f I. Paris and London will be 3fl apart. The Parisian Exhibition of 190 was to h%ve had this model earth as an attraction, but monetary considerations have c tutted the idea to be given up, and now M. tteclus has come to London to see what can be done. To give tac:lities for examining every part conveni- ently, a circular staging is to be erected round a circular bath or pond filled with water, and in this will float the globe, which wilt be hollow. Very little force will then be usces- s .ry, to bring part after part to the level of the spectatm's eyes.

HALLAM-POTTS.

------- - - --- On Thursday last. week: the marriage of Miss Ethel Mary Potts, the only daughter of the Rev. MARRIA.GE OF MR. ARTHUR PORRITT.

A wedding of some local interest was celebrated in St. Philip's Church, Maidstone, on Thursday last week, when Mr: Arthur Porritt, of London, younger son of Mr. John Porritt, of Warrington, and Miss Madeliene ,May Mary Day,eldest daughter of the late Mr. Prank Day, of Maidstone, were united in the bonds of holy matrimony. The offici- ating clergyman was the Rev. H. Collin, M.A., and the ceremony was witnessed by a number ot rela- tions and friends, same of whom had travelled from London and Warrington. The bride who was given away by her mother was charmingly, attired in a dram or white silk trimmed with tulle and chiffon and sprays of orange blossom. She carried a beauti- ful shower bouquet, the gilt of the bridegroom. The three bridesmaids in attendance were Miss Daisy Day (sister of the brido)and the Misses Phair. Their dresses were of white-spottod muslin over pale-blue silk with tichus and blue sashes,and hats to match. They also carried exquisite bouquets. The duties of best man were satisfactorily fulfilled by Mr. Woodall, of London. Appropriate voluntaries were played on the organ by Mr. Henniker :during the assembling of the wedding party in the church. and at the conclusion of the ceremony he rendered Mendefasohn's "Wedding March." The wedding breakfast was served at the house of the bride's mother when about fifty guests were present. Tbe Rev. Griffith Jones of Balham in proposing the toast of "the Bride and Bridegroom" gave suitable expression to the feelings of those present; and the bridegroom brirfly responded. During the afternoon a photograph of the wedding party was taken, and when the newly•rearried couple left for Scarborough, where the honeymoon is being spent, they were greeted with showers of confetti and rice. They were the recipients of a large number of choice wedding presents.'

ANGLICAN ROMANIS : B ALP TISMAL "REGENERATION.

Sir.—Kindly permit me to thank your correspondent, " Sursum Corda," for his learned disquisition on " Bap- tismal Regeneration." I must,noweyer, decline the op- portunity which it presents for the discussion of a dogma for which I have no sympathy. As you sir, are aware, my lel ter undPr the above heading %cal absolutely nen- contmtious : I neither sided with ncr a,talnst the d la- trine in que,tion. The purport of my communicati n was to shew,trorn official author ties.that in the mater of Baptismal Regeneration the Churches of Rome and Canterbury were of one mini. But were I asked about my views of Baptism I should Fay that I incline to the intelligerA and manly doc!rine of the public immers.on of 'Relievers as commanded by Christ and carried out by fig Apostles. The alleged regeneration and salvation of men through a religious ceremony erroneously called in- fant baptism seems a strange thing to noe,sFeing that the parties most interested in the proneedings knew nothing at all about them. _ _ _

Until I read your correspondent's letter I wag list aware that Diasenters ware bel,evers in Baptism4l Re- generation. I knew inded that most of them sprinkled their children and called the process biptiim ; but I did not know that, like their Anglican and Roman brethran, they attached soul-saying qualities to tha coremany.- -Yours truly, 'IONA.

TUESDAY.

_ The pastoral sessian was resumed in Hull on Tuesday, the President in the chair. The conference, after some preliminary business, passed to the memorial service for ministers who had died during the year. The names of those who have died were read by the secretary. A significant adittion was made by the Rev. Enoch Salt, who intimated the receipt of a telegram announcing the death of Dr. Young,until recently chairrcaea of the Bir- mingham Distr ct and Governor of the Theological In- stitution at Bandsworth. The reading of the names was followed by a succession of deeply sympathetic re- ferences to the men who had fallen during the year hi' I-some of their colleagues and friends. —The Rev. J. 3. Simon brought up the report of the committee on the memorial from the Bolton district and various circuits, asking that no persons directly engaged in the manufac- ture or sale of intoxicating liquors should be elected to official positions of the church. The committee reported that while they thoroughly sympathised with the tem- perance work, this proposal was not in harmony with their rules, as interfering with the autho- rity of the superintendents and circuits.— Rev. J. S. Simon proposed the adoption of the com- mittee's repert, and was supported by the Rev. G. A. Bennetts, temp lance secmary. Mr. Bennetts, in sup- poring the r-commendation of the committ -e, said he hated the drink ; but he could not go so far as the dolton resolution. Hetil he could say it was a sin to drink and buy, he could not say it was wrung to The Rev. Thomas Champness raid he was exceedingly anxious for peace. They could only rake one step at a . time ; bt the re could be no abiding peace between Christ and &Fat. They must warn their pe3ple against complicity in the drink traffic.—Dr. Stephenson said that in view of what must be called an attempt to force the hand of this and other c iurchs, it was ienp or- tent that they should present a uhited front as to their relation to the liquor traffic.- -After some farther discussion, the conference unani- mously adopted a resolution to the effect that the con- ference could not in' erfere with the constitutional form of electing church officers; but, at the same time, rec3m- mends their people to keep free from complicity in a traffic the results of which were so injurious to religion,' morality, and social life. Another subject on which varicus mmnorials had been sent was the enlargement of the membership of the representative seseion. The memorial was f llowed by a no ice of motion by the ' Rev. E. Arnaud Sc4tt, who moved t at the repro elate- ties session he eo enlarged as t 3 consist of 300 ministers and 300 lee m,n. The metien was supp:rted by the Rev. J.FI. Moult ui.—The ex-President said there may not be much difference of opinion on the main quesnon. It was mrra aqu stion of opportuueness. CYCLING.

The Daily News says:—For Mr. Platt-Betts, the famous cycling champion who mat with so severe an accident oil the trick about a month ago, will break no more records. Statements have appeared in some of the cycling papers to the effect that he intends riding NZ Ain this season. As a mater of fact,it is impossib!e that he can ever ride again, aad it is sail doubtful whether he will recover from his terrib:e Injuries. His skull is brok:n and he has a compound frac'mrs of the jawbone. He underwent a sur4ical operation to the jaw on Thursday, and those who have seen him describe hi i condition as terrible. Is it nit time that these attempts to break records sh mid ce,s,‘ now that the pace is so terriffia that the least mishap causes suffering and disfivarement for life, even when death is not the result ? In Mr. Platt- Betts's case, it will be remembered, the htudle-bar of a triplet machine ridden by his pacemakers came out of the socket,and one of the riders svaq shot as from a cata- pult on to the top of a covered stand. Mr. Platt-Batts himself was clashed violently to the ground, then shot high up into the air, then shot on the graund again. No wonder, seeing that to beat the, mile record nowadays one has to go at the rate of an express train.

Ta a certain ex'ent the policeman is regarded by eyclists in the light of an enemy, or as an enemy to cycling. The writer's experience this last week wti one of that mueh maligned body of men may be of interest. Riding down a road about fourteen miles from Warring- ton my lamp went out. I found there was no oil in the lamp whatever, and determined as it was very late at night to risk the run. There was a fall moon and only an oceisional tramp on the road,so I thought thecircum- stances just ifi,cl my purpose. I proceeded on,and after travelling a couple of miles passed a policeman by about 20 yards before he saw me. 11,1 hailed me and asked me if I wanted some oil in my lamp. I replied in the afiir- ! mative, and at his request went with him to h.s howl + close by,where he otligingly filled my lamp with o I.He ' told me he bad a long walk before him during his night patrol and showed his appreciation of the similarity of our positions and his gold beartednass at the Mime time by filling a couple of glasses with a light " refresher." Need I say that this was very acceptable after covering about forty miles. During the course of a short conver- sation he informed me that he was a cyclist. Would that explain all his good Samaritanism ? Or was he the eacep,ion th.t proves toe rule ? 1 would much prefer to think not, with regard to the latter query.

On Bank Holiday A. A. Cnaso made a successful attempt on the world' nile record (11 'paced), 10,-a,lup.: previous time— 2i*n. 3 2-54ec—by 4-5 h c. His tine for the quarter miles were: First quarter, 304,c. ; half- mile, 59 2-siPc. ; threa-quarters, Imm. 10-35iec. ; and the full mile in Inin. 2 3-5030.1.

Sire is a tip for tilers who use pneumatic brakei. Whencvi.r circumstances seem to point towards the necessity for a rapid use of the brake, pump just a little air to, so that should an emsrgen;y arise the first squeeze of the bulb puts the brake oa tyre, oth-ir wise, hitless yin have the brake shoe very Close to the tyre. the unexpntel may have happenvil before you have actually got the brake on. One great advantage of this brake when fitted to light machines is that it cannot be applied too rapidiy,aud yet it mty be used as quickly as is safe 03 any bicycle, provided you act up to the tip given above.

' Edwards, the Liverpool rider, is still piling up his 103 miles a day in the States. Be is now riding at Chicago, and in an interview states that he has fallen in love with tnat city, and will ride ther3 tor several weeks longer. He says he never reatis)cl what good roads were until he struck the Coicago boulevards. In another in- terview Edwards said, " I do not scorch. I take rnv time and stop for a smoke, and a rest every time I want to. Lately I have been r;ding from the Chicago Beach Hotel to Evanston and return, making the trip twice, and finishing the hundred on the boulevards."

What a satisfaction it is to open one's mind about the condition of a road whsn cycang. Travelling atoms the other day in the neighbourhood of Bolton I had coversd some fi17.0 or six milks of badly laid sets, which were little batter than cobble stones, and my tyres being well ieB tted, the shaking I g,t can bs we'l imagined. I slipped off the machine as I got up to a knot of people who were conversing together by the road side, and addre-Aing them quite &Ode-11y said "Why on card.' don't you have proper road 4 out lasre ?'' I never saw any me more astonis'iel thin that crowd applaled with their mouths open, as I mour.t xl an I rode on. The act and nmark were quite spontaneous born of ths impulse to open my mind lo someone.

DA.E[LiA qUEEN FESTIVAL AT BURTON WOOD.

The Lurth annual feitiv al in connection with the coronation of the Dahlia Qusea, took place at Burton- wood on Monday in beautiful weather, and under most favourable circumstances. rhe affair was inaugurated by Mr. G. Anders, of the Bridge Inn, Burtonwood, and is still carried on by that gentleman with the assistance of his son and others. It is looked forward to with considerable interest by the villagers, and they put for- ward every effort to make it a succeis, by turning their children out in a presentable manner. Still it cannot truthfully be said that this year's demonstration was any advancement in point of display on last year, and 1 tha procession carried with it a sameness, s hich was rather Wsappointing. The time announced for the pro- cession to leave the field where the muster took place was 11-30, but there did not appear to be any undue i effort to make a start until over an hour afterwards, whoa something like order was obtained. Soon after 12-30, Mr. Jas. Kilshaw, as mounted Marshall, emerged from the field and immediately behind him came the Morris dancers, led by a youth name John Wall, and they performed and danced along the route of the procession. Parr St Peter's band followed, and immediately behind were 24 girls as skipping rope dancers trained by Mr. J. H. Anders. A youthful " Napoleon "on horseback headed "N galleon's" carriage conveying the Queen (Miss Edith Seplron) and her maids of honour. Following the carriage came the crown bearer, sword bearer and sceptre bearer, and a squad of Highland soldiers. Neat came a carriage cony lying the ex-queen (Miss Evelyn Fairclough). Britannia was represented by Miss A. GrifFl,:hs, surrounded by England, Ireland, Scotland. and Wales. Then followed a gathering of Littk Red Mina Hoods, seamstresses, flower girls, colliers, cow boys, nurses, a Primrose Queen, white slave, and oth,,r char- acters, including a couple of comic cyclistl.the re or being brought up by D. Houghton's lodge, Bridge Inn branch of the Order of Druids, with banner. Toe procersion paraded the village and environs and proceeded to a field near Collins Green, lent by Mr. Fairclougt. Here the ceremony of crowning the queen was per- formed by Mrs. R. Forshaw, who in honour of the occasion presented her with a hand-bag haying gold I cased mountings. The throne presented a very neat and tasteful appearance and reflected credit upon Mr. J. Brown, of E srlestown, under whose supervision it had been erected. On th, staging about the throne were noticed Mr. and Mrs. R. Forshaw, Mr. and Mrs. Fair- dough (Brook Head), Mr. and Mrs. Southern, Mr. and Mrs. J. Sephten. Mr. W. Webster, Mr. Warburton, and Mrs. Martha Boardman (Culcheth), 94 years of age, the oldest lady in the village, and on the field there were about 3,000 persons. The festivities attendant on the coronation included the plaiting of the Dahlia pale, by 24 boys and girls, trained by Mr. J. H. Anders, skipping rope dancers and exhibition jumping by Mr. Burke. Prizes were awarded for the neatest and cleanest turn- outs,as follows.—Tradesmen's class Ist Mr. J. Ratcliffe, Sutton ; 2nd Mr. John Price, Sutton ; 3rd Mr. Burke, baker, Sutton ; Heavy horse and furry —lit Mr. R. Naylor, Pear Tree Hotel,Collins Green; 2nd Israel Smith; 3rd Mr. John Fairclough, Yew Tree farm, Bartonwood. Tho prizes for comic cyclists were awarded to W. Pro- bert (Weary Willie and Tired Tim.) For neatest cyclists Mr. Austin, Eirlestown. There were several side shows on the field which were well patronised by the visitors. On Tuesday all the children who took part in the dem- onstration were entertained to tea on the field by Mr. G. Anders. and were given free admission to the side shows. Each child: was also presented :by Mr. Anders with a piece of silver.

OUR. LOCAL CHURCHES.

EMMANUEL REFORMED CHURCH OF ENGLAND.

At this church on Sunlai—e-vn-rain-g a sermon was preached by the Rev. A. M. Fiubly. rector of S. John's Reformed Ep's3opal Church, Sussex, New Brunswick, Canada. As one of the delegates from the province of New Brunswick, Mr. Hubly at ended the Worl i's Con- vention of Sunday SMools, recently held in London, at which 2,500 reprasentatives of Sunday School Associa- tions were present from all parts of the globe.

Thomas Long, twenty-five ysars of age, a native of Swan- sea, was drowned whilst bathing on Tuesday night on the Swansea sands.

A boat containlnz four men capsized on Tuesday at Raker, Sunderland, and George William Clark, who became entan- gled in the rear, was drowned. While John Rimmer, employed in the carriage department of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Railway at Chapel-street Station. Southport, was following his usual avocation on Tuesday, he found in one of the compartments a packet con- taining £156 in banknotes, postal orders, and coin. The valuables were banded over to Mr. Hughes, the eta ion master and on Wednesday were claimed by a Manx gentleman, who has promised a suitable reward to the finder. William Greaves Edwards were sentenced at the Birming. ham Assizes to ten years penal servitude for the attempted I murder or his daughter by throwing her under a train at New-street Station on April 6th. 1 Thomas Jones. a hawker, aged thirty-five, was executed at I Carnarvon on Wednesday morning for the murder of Mary Bruton, also a hawker, on a mountain near Llan Festiniog, Merionethshire. Seventy houses have been destroyed and forty lives lost at Villa Medians, in Spain, owing to a waterspout breaking over the town. Many villages were destroye I in a subse- quent inundation. The four-masted barque Ecclefechan, of Liverpe9l, was re- por.,ed ashore on Wednesday at Dunvorley, on the Irish coast.

At the Liverpool Assizes, John Almond, described as a musician, was sentenced to two years' hard labour for mis- conduct with choir boys at Sefton. On Tuesday Herbert Deacon, ft carter boy employed on a farm at Shoreham, near Brighton, was in charge of three horses attached to a reaping machine when the horses bolted. Deanna, In trying to stop thorn, was knocked down and killed, the reaping machine inflicting fearful cuts about the bead and body. The Admiralty, according to a St Tohn'e telegram, have deeided to enrol 600 Newfoundland fishermen in the Naval Reserve. The Chief Constable of Chester. Mr. G. L. Fenwick, has tendered his resignation, after filling the office since 1864. A shocking accident occurred at Willesden Junction on Tuesday morning. About 7.45. as the Scotch express was leaving the platform for Ruston, a newsboy in the employ of Messrs. W. H. Smith and Son. whilst serving a passenger in the front sleeping saloon, slipped and was caught between the footboard and the platform. He was decapitated before any elf eetual assistance could be rendered.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT].

Bank Holiday in the district was observed in the usual way by the inhabitants indulging in their customary seaside trips, and large numbers availed themselves of the chesp excursions run to all parts of the country. Blackpool, Southport. Liverpool and North Wales were the general favourites.

The success of the Halton-road Choir at Formby, is another feather in the cap of its musical conduc- tor (Mr. R. Yates), and is a feat for which he can justly take credit. The success was highly deserved, inasmuch as the whele choir entered into the spirit of the competition with no half-heartedness, and can be attributed to nothing but hard practice.

August the 17th has been fixed as the date upon which the Cycle Parade is to take place, and this year the event promises to excel all previous efforts. The Runcorn Cycle Club who are the pioneers in the movement, are allowing nothing to stand in the way ; already they have done a great amount, of work in the way of preparation. The object is the raising of funds for the Cottage Hospital, the value of which will be inestimable in a district like Run- corn.

The local Lodge of Masons. who know. how to enjoy themselves, visited Llanrwst, Trefriw, the Swallow Falls, and Battwi-y-coed, on .rtiurs lay week, for their annual picnic. The weather was delightful, and the charming scenery through which they passed was fully appreciated.

The dangers of leaving hot water anywhere in the vicinity of children can never be too over-rated. as Is illustrated by the sad circumstances attending the death of a little boy named Gayter, (reported in another column). Here,the mother, apprehending no danger, left a large pan of hot water on the floor for a few moments ; but those moments were fatal. The little fellow staggered backwards into the pan and was so severely scalded, that he died from the effects on Saturday, after over a week of terrible suffering.

The Runcorn tam on Monday (Bank Ho:iday) indulged in their usual fixture with the Old Tottenhallians at Runcorn. The game was an all day match, which for a number of years Past has resulted in the defeat of the hornesters. The result on this occasion was a draw, the " Old Tats" scor- ing 155 for two innings, whilst the Runcorn eleven put up 108 in the first innings, and 57 for 6 wickets on the second essay. Cipt,iin Cross showed excel- lent form for his " not out 32,"

The visitors had some excellent bats with them, but they evidently must have been a trifle hand'- capped as they had to play three substitutes, which was a little disappointing. Their sixth wicket fell for 97, and the tenth for 93 in the first innings, but on the second attempt the " tail " got on a little better, and put on 20 between them. The sixth wicket falling for 37 and the 10th for 57.

At the meeting of the Runcorn Council. on Thursday morning, the chief subject under discus- sion was the consideration of the conversion of the Market Hall into public baths. The discussion upon the subject was a very animated one,and may prove very useful, but at the same time there was too great a display of feeling in the whole proceed- ings to secure any real go.od,and it is fortunate that cooler minds prevailed in getting the decision on so important a subject deferred for a little time. There was .a little danger in the passin,z of a scheme, which, whilst conveying a boon with one hand was at the same time depriving the neigh- bourhood of another. Just fancy £2300! why the most famous—mini not the biggest—bath in the whole British Isles only cost a little over £lOOO to complete, and still they accommodate more bathers than any baths known. Slimly a place in which to have a really enjoyable bathe can be had at. a cheaper rate, and if cheaper, it need not necessarily be any nastier. Besides as Mr.Handley pointed out there is very little wisdom in having too many irons in the fire. The scheme itself may provide a good bath for £2301; but there is a possibility of having both a good bath and a good Market to show for £2,000 or less

THE SALVATION ARMY.

On Monday, several members of the Earlestown branch of the Salvation Army joined another corps at Huyton in celebration of the 33rd anniversary of the foundation of the Salvation Army. The pro- ceedings were held in a field near Huyton Station, kindly lent by Mr. Lightfoot and hero the usual formula of the Army was carried through. After- wards the members indulged in a little enjoyment, refreshments being served during the day. The Earlestown contingent reached home about half- past nine. Tim VULCAN FOUNDRY PICNIC CLUB.

On Saturday about 26 members of the above club left Earlestown for Bakewell. The journey to War- rington Cantral Station was made by omnibus and from there the 8.48. a.m., train was taken to Bake- well. Breakfast was partaken of at the Castle Hotel and afterwards the party journeyed by means of avaggonettas to Haddon Hall and were shown through by the lady guide, much interest being taken in the old oak carvings. Chatsworth House was the next place of interest visited but the party were disappointed in not being able to go through as the house was closed, but a drive was taken round the grounds. On returning to the Castle Hotel dinner was partaken of. After spending a very enjoyable day,the party left Bake,well at about 9 o'clock, and arrived in Earlestown at 12.30. p.m. I -

VIADUCT WORKS SMITHY PICNIC.

Several of the men employed in the ain'ithies the Viaduct Works, Earlestown, to the number of 44, had their annual picnic on Monday, the destina- tion this year being Chatsworth. The party met on the market ground at 515 in the morning,and drove in wagonettes to St. Mary's Station, Lowton, Great Central Railway, where they entrained for Sheffield. On arrival they drove to Moorhead restaurant where a capital breakfast was partaken of. Wagonettes were again chartered and an enjoy- able drive commenced through the beautiful Dore Valley, and over the Moors to Froggart Edge from whence views of the lovely scenery were obtained. A call was made at Devonshire Arms, Bestow, for baiting the horses. After a short rest the members of the party had a walk through the Park in the direction of Chataworth,the mansion of the Duke of Devonshire, Atter viewing the interior of the house they returned to the Devonshire Arms,where a first class table d'hot6 was served and apprecia- ted. At the conclusion of the meal they commenced the homeward journey driving through Totley to Sheffield, making a circular drive 0(26 miles. The weather was beautifully fine, and the party spent a very pleasant day. The whole of the arrangements were undertaken by Messrs. Dean and Dawson.

THE PROPOSED ELECTRIC TRAMS AT ST. HELENS.

The St. Helens Corporation Electric Lighting and Traction Contmittee have adopted a new time table for the tramways on the inauguration of electrical traction, which is now being introduced. The Prescot section will consist practically of a half-hourly service through- out the day with a 15 minutes' service to St. Ann's, There will be 15 minutes' service on the Denton's-green and Peasley-cross sections, and a twenty minutes' ser- vice to and from Haydock.

SCIENCS: AND ART EXAMINATIONS AT ST.. HELENS.

At a meeting of th-isitlielen-ii.-Technical Education Committee the director (Mr. J. J. Broomhead) reported that the results of the recent examinations had come to band. In science subjects 247 candidates were presen- ted, of whom 153 passed, against 239 and 160, respec- tively, in the previous year ; in art subjects there were 137 candidates and 56 passes. against 151 and 110, res- pectively, last year ; in the City and Guilds of London Institute examinations there were 30 candidates and 18 passes against, 32 and 22, respectively, last year ; and in the Union of Lancashire and Crieshire Institutes ex- aminathns there were 177 candidates and 170 passes, against 97 and 87 last year.

STRIKING AN OFFICER.

The Southern District orders of yesterday -contain the confirmation by tho War Office of a sentence of five years penal servitude and discharge with ignominy frons her Majesty's service passed hy a recent court martial upon Private Charles Warren, of the Elighlanu Light Di - ?entry for striking Lieutenant Prior, of the Royal Engineer Militia, across the face with his cap at Shore- cliffe Camp on June 11.

PRINCE BISMARB, FUNERAL SERVICE AT BERLIN.

In BERLIN, THURSDAY. vieW Or the funeral service for the late Prince Bismark held this morning in the Emporcr Memorial Church there are siems or mourning on eve*, hand. A terrible storm occurred at Villa Mediana en Tuee' day. A waterspout broke over the town, and destroyed seventy houses. The inhabitants were taken by set; prise. Forty were drowned by the sudden rush oa water. Many other 3 had to seek refuge on the roofs of houses. Help was sent from Burgos. in Palencia, and assistance rendered to the distressed townsfolk. 0,12 Wednesday the storm again visited the neiehbourhooa• A small valley was inunaated, and many villages Were destroyed. BOLTON PLITgBERS LOCKED OCT. The opesative plumbers in Bolton, to the number of 80, were locked out yesterday morning owing to a pec,dew liar dispute. The men at one shop refused to work the employes of a Liverpool firm of metalworkel whereupon the employers on Tuesday decided to lock ,_°ll,, the whole town, because the men had struck this BOY without giving the usual notice. No intimation wag received from the operatives on Wednesday night, and they were all locked out yesterday. BRUTAL MURDER OF AN IRISH GIRL. A murder of a particularly brutal character has Wen committed at Ballynoe, Rileare. A young girl named Fanny Harris, the daughter of a farmer,was found des on Wednesday, in a lane connecting two public roade. Her head had been battered in, and near at hand wag a large stone stained with blood. It is stated that the poor girl was outraged before beinganurdered. No arrestS have been made.
